implies that the pullback along the covering map
\mathrm{Spec}(k)\to X^{\operatorname{HT}}\otimes k
identifies
\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}}\otimes k)
with
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{B}}(k[\theta])
, where
\mathcal{B}
is the full subcategory of the category of (discrete)
k[\theta]
-modules consisting of those objects which are
\theta
-power torsion (see
[
Sta
,
Tag 05E6
]
for the precise definition) and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{B}}(k[\theta])
is the full subcategory of
\mathcal{D}(k[\theta])
whose objects are those
M\in\mathcal{D}(k[\theta])
such that
\mathrm{H}^{n}(M)\in\mathcal{B}
. Moreover, under such an identification
\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}}\otimes k)^{\heartsuit}
is matched with
\mathcal{B}
. Hence for our purpose, it is enough to check that the natural map
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{B}}(k[\theta])
is an equivalence, which follows from
[
Sta
,
Tag 0955
]
.
∎

Given
Proposition

3.1.20
, the discussion in this subsection so far can be summarized as follows:

Proposition 3.1.21
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the pullback along
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(A/d^{n})\to X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
induces a functor

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})&\to\mathcal{D}(A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])\\
\mathcal{E}&\mapsto(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E},\partial_{\mathcal{E}})\end{split}

which will be denoted as
\beta_{n}^{+}
later. Here
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
is defined after
Corollary

3.1.10
.

Proof.

By the discussion above
Lemma

3.1.11
, for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})^{\heartsuit}
,
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
is an
A
-module concentrated on degree
0
equipped with an operator
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}:\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
. Moreover,
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
satisfies the Leibnitz rule stated in
Lemma

3.1.11
, hence
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
can be viewed as a (left)
A[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]
-module, whose underlying module is
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
with
\partial
acting via
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
. Consequently it induces a functor
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})^{\heartsuit})\to\mathcal{D}(A[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])
. By
Proposition

3.1.20
and functoriality, it coincides with the functor stated in the proposition.
∎

Remark 3.1.22
.

Recall that in
[
BL22a
]
, the Sen operator for complexes on the Hodge-Tate locus is defined on the complex itself, while our
q
-connection is defined on
\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E})
other than on
\mathscr{E}
itself, this is because our construction of the isomorphism
\gamma_{b,c}
relies on certain coordinates
q
in the
q
-prism when
n>1
. This is a feature but not a bug as we expect that the
q
-connection
\partial
for the structure sheaf should act nontrivially on
q
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. Recall that for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the global section of
\mathcal{E}
is defined as

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E}):=\lim_{f:\mathrm{Spec}(R)\to X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}}f^{*}\mathcal{E}

In particular, the cover
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(A/I^{n})\rightarrow X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
induces a natural morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}.

The next proposition shows that it actually factors through the fiber of
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proposition 3.1.23
.

For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the natural morphism
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
induces a canonical morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\mathcal{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}).

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.1.9
and
Corollary

3.1.10
, we have an isomorphism
\gamma_{b,c}:\rho\circ\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho
as functors
\mathrm{Spf}(R)\rightarrow X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
. Then the definition of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})
implies that the natural morphism
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
factors through the equalizer of

\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Id}\otimes 1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{A/d^{n}}R/d^{n}

and

\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{b,c}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{A/d^{n},\psi}R/d^{n},

where
\gamma_{b,c}=\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
. This produces a canonical morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\mathscr{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}).

∎

Remark 3.1.24
.

Eventually we will show the above morphism actually identifies
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})
with
\operatorname{fib}(\partial_{\mathcal{E}})
. For that purpose, we need to investigate the behavior of
\partial
on the Hodge-Tate locus first, which is the task of the next subsection.

3.2.
q
-connections on the Hodge-Tate locus

Now we study the isomorphism
\gamma_{b,c}
and hence the behavior of the
q
-connection
\partial
when restricted to the Hodge-Tate locus (i.e. take
n=1
in
Corollary

3.1.10
) in more detail. First notice that
\psi:A/d\to R/d
coincides with the natural inclusion as
\psi(x)=x,\forall x\in A/d
, we see
\gamma_{b,c}
descends to an automorphism of

\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\operatorname{HT}}\times_{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})}\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon)),

for simplicity here and in the following in this subsection we still write
q
for its image in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, which is actually
\zeta_{p^{\alpha+1}}
. This implies that for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
,
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
also descends to a functor from
\mathcal{E}
to itself.

As
\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\operatorname{HT}}
is the classifying stack of
G_{\pi}
over
\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
by
[
BL22b
, Proposition 9.5]
, where
G_{\pi}
is calculated in
[
BL22b
, Example 9.6]
. More explicitly
13
13

13

Here we emphasize that although the calculation in
[
BL22b
, Proposition 9.5, Example 9.6]
used the Breuil-Kisin prism, one could check by hand that it still works with the
q
-prism instead. In particular, the
E^{\prime}(\pi)
in
loc. cit.
could be replaced with the derivative of
d=[p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}}
(with respect to
q
), denoted as
d^{\prime}(q)
later.

G_{\pi}=\{(t,a)\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}\ltimes\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}\ |\ t-1=d^{\prime}(q)\cdot a\}

Under this identification,
\gamma_{b,c}
corresponds to an element in
G_{\pi}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon))
, and we claim this element is precisely
(1+d^{\prime}(q)\epsilon,\epsilon)
.
14
14

14

Here by our definition we see that
\epsilon^{k}=\epsilon q^{k-1}(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)^{k-1}
. In particular, it doesn’t vanish for
k\geq 1
, which is a key difference from its analog in the Breuil-Kisin setting studied in
[
AHB22
]
. However, the divided powers of
\epsilon
still exist in
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon)
as
v_{p}(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)=\frac{1}{p-1}
, hence
v_{p}(n!)\leq\frac{n-1}{p-1}=v_{p}(q^{n-1}(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)^{n-1})
. Consequently,
\epsilon\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon))
.
.
To see this, unwinding the construction of
\gamma_{b,c}
from
b
and
c
, we just need to verify that the image of
b
(resp.
c
) under the natural morphism

W(A[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon))\to W(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon))

lies in
\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon))
(resp.
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon))
) and is precisely given by
1+d^{\prime}(q)\epsilon
(resp.
\epsilon
). As
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon)
is
p
-torsion free, it suffices to check that
b_{0}=1+d^{\prime}(q)\epsilon
and
c_{0}=\epsilon
in
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\epsilon q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\epsilon)
, which are both clear from our construction of
b
and
c
in
Proposition

3.1.5
(see
Remark

3.1.6
) and
Proposition

3.1.8
.

For simplicity we rename
d^{\prime}(q)\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
as
e
and denote
q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)
as
\beta\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
. First we would like to calculate
e
explicitly.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

e\beta=p^{\alpha+1}
.

Proof.

By definition,
e=\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}ip^{\alpha}q^{ip^{\alpha}-1}=\frac{p^{\alpha}}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}i(q^{p^{\alpha}})^{i}
. Let
t=q^{p^{\alpha}}
and
S=\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}it^{i}
, then

tS-S=\sum_{i=2}^{p}(i-1)t^{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}it^{i}=pt^{p}-\sum_{i=1}^{p}t^{i}=p,

hence
e=\frac{p^{\alpha}}{q}\cdot\frac{p}{q^{p^{\alpha}}-1}=\frac{p^{\alpha+1}}{\beta}
.
∎

The next lemma helps us understand how
\partial
acts on
\rho^{*}\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
under the covering morphism
\rho:X\to X^{\operatorname{HT}}
, in parallel to the behavior of the Sen operator
\theta
studied in
[
BL22a
]
and
[
AHB22
]
(see
[
AHB22
, Lemma 2.6]
for details).

Lemma 3.2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
, we have the following

•

\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong\mathcal{O}_{G_{\pi}}\cong\widehat{\bigoplus}_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\frac{a^{n}}{n!}
.

•

Suppose that
p>2
or
p=2
and
\alpha>0
, then the sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}_{K}\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\partial}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\to 0
is exact.

Proof.

The projection formula tells us that
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong\mathcal{O}_{G_{\pi}}
. Moreover, the projection
G_{\pi}\to\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}
sending
(t,a)
to
a
identifies
G_{\pi}
with
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}
, under which the group law on
G_{\pi}
is transferred to the following formal group law on
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
:

\Delta:\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}\hat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}},~~~~a\mapsto a+b+e\cdot ab.

Under the above identification, the isomorphism
\gamma_{b,c}
(hence also the
q
-connection
\partial
) is just constructed via
\epsilon\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon))
, which precisely means that for
f(a)\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
,
\gamma_{b,c}(f)=f+\epsilon\partial(f)=f(a+\epsilon+ea\epsilon)
, hence
\epsilon\partial(f)=f(a+\epsilon+ea\epsilon)-f(a)
.

First we show that
\ker(\partial)=\mathcal{O}_{K}
, i.e.
\partial(f)=0
if and only if
f
is a constant function. One direction is obvious. On the other hand, if
\partial(f)=0
, then
f(a)\equiv f(a+\epsilon+ea\epsilon)
. Take
a=0
, we see that
g(\epsilon)=0
for
g(a):=f(a)-f(0)
. Via induction we could produce a sequence
\{y_{n}\}
such that
y_{0}=0
,
y_{n}=(e\epsilon+1)^{n}(\epsilon+\frac{1}{e})-\frac{1}{e}
(hence
y_{i}\neq y_{j}
for
i\neq j
), such that
g(y_{i})=0
for all
i
. As all of calculations happen at
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon)
, hence still hold in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, which is
(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon))/(\epsilon-\beta)
. Consequently we get a sequence
\{z_{n}\}
in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
such that
z_{0}=0,z_{n}=(e\beta+1)^{n}(\beta+\frac{1}{e})-\frac{1}{e}=\frac{(p^{\alpha+1}+1)^{n+1}-1}{e}
and
g(z_{n})=0
. Let
t_{n}=\frac{z_{n}}{\beta}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
and define
\kappa(a)
to be
g(\beta a)
. Then
\kappa(a)\in\mathcal{O}_{K}\langle a\rangle
and
\kappa(t_{n})=0,\forall n\geq 0
. An easy application of the Weierstrass preparation theorem (as
t_{i}\neq t_{j}
for
i\neq j
) implies that
\kappa\equiv 0
, which also implies the vanishing of
g
, hence
f
must be a constant function.

Next we prove that
\partial
is surjective. We first observe that
\partial
is
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear. Indeed, this follows from the Leibniz rule given in
Lemma

3.1.11
and the vanishing of
\partial
on
A/d=\mathcal{O}_{K}
. Given
g\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
, we wish to construct
f\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
such that
\partial(f)=g
. We do this by induction modulo
\pi^{n}
for the uniformizer
\pi=q-1
. More precisely, we wish to construct a sequence
\{h_{i}\}_{i\geq 1}
such that
h_{i}\in\pi^{i-1}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
and
f_{i}:=\sum_{k=1}^{i}f_{k}
satisfies that
\partial(h_{i})\equiv g
modulo
\pi^{i}
, then
f:=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}f_{i}
converges and is a solution for
\partial(f)=g
. Assuming the statement holds for
i=1
for a moment, then we could do the induction as follows. Suppose
k\geq 1
and we have find
h_{i}
’s up to
i=k
satisfying the desired properties, then
\partial(f_{k})-g=\pi^{k}l_{k}
for some
l_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
by assumption, where
f_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}h_{i}
. By the base case, there exists
h_{k+1}^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
such that
\partial(h_{k+1}^{\prime})\equiv-l_{k}
mod
\pi
. Hence if we take
h_{k+1}=\pi^{k}h_{k+1}^{\prime}
, then
\partial(f_{k+1})-g=\pi^{k}(l_{k}+h^{\prime}_{k})\in\pi^{k+1}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
for
f_{k+1}=f_{k}+h_{k+1}
. We thus finish the induction process.

Now we focus on the base case and would like to show that for any
g\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
, there exists
f\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
such that
\partial(f)\equiv g
modulo
\pi
. It might be tempting to apply
[
AHB22
, Lemma 2.6]
directly as
\epsilon^{2}=\beta\epsilon=0
after modulo
\pi
. However, this doesn’t work as the higher divided powers of
\epsilon
don’t vanish. Indeed, since
v_{p}(\beta)=\frac{1}{p-1}
,
\frac{\epsilon^{p^{n}}}{p^{n}!}=\frac{\beta^{n-1}}{n!}\epsilon\neq 0
in
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}/\pi
for any
n\geq 1
. For our purpose, we need to analyze
\partial(f)
in more detail. First we observe that for
f=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\frac{a^{n}}{n!}\in\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}
,

\begin{split}\gamma_{b,c}(f)&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\frac{(a+(1+ea)\epsilon)^{n}}{n!}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}(a^{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n}{i}a^{n-i}(1+ea)^{i}\epsilon^{i})\\
&=f(a)+\epsilon\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\binom{n}{i}a^{n-i}(1+ea)^{i}\beta^{i-1}).\end{split}

On the other hand, since
\gamma_{b,c}(f)=f+\epsilon\partial(f)
, hence

\begin{split}\partial(f)&=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{n}\frac{(a+\beta ea+\beta)^{n}-a^{n}}{n!}=\frac{1}{\beta}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}(((1+\beta e)^{n}-1)a^{n}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{i}(1+\beta e)^{i}\beta^{n-i}a^{i})\\
&=\frac{1}{\beta}(\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(\sum_{n=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}\binom{n}{i}(1+\beta e)^{i}\beta^{n-i})a^{i})\\
&=\frac{1}{\beta}((\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}\beta_{n}}{n!})+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}(\frac{c_{i}}{i!}((1+\beta e)^{i}-1)+\sum_{n=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}\binom{n}{i}(1+\beta e)^{i}\beta^{n-i})a^{i})\end{split}

Consequently if we let
\partial(f)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}b_{i}\frac{a^{i}}{i!}
, then by comparing the coefficients, we have that

\begin{split}b_{i}=\frac{1}{\beta}(c_{i}((1+\beta e)^{i}-1)+(1+\beta e)^{i}(f^{(i)}(\beta)-c_{i}))=\frac{1}{\beta}(-c_{i}+(1+\beta e)^{i}f^{(i)}(\beta)).\end{split}

From now on we work in
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}/\pi
. As
p>2
or
\alpha>0
,
v_{p}(e)=(\alpha+1)-\frac{1}{p-1}>0
, which implies that

(3.7)

b_{i}=\frac{1}{\beta}(-c_{i}+f^{(i)}(\beta))=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}c_{n+i}\frac{\beta^{n-1}}{n!}=\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}c_{p^{t}+i}u_{p^{t}},

where
u_{p^{t}}:=\frac{\beta^{p^{t}-1}}{p^{t}!}
is a unit. Here the last equality follows from the fact that
v_{p}(\beta)=\frac{1}{p-1}
.

Our goal is to show that for a fixed sequence
\{b_{i}\}
,
b_{i}\in k
and
b_{N}=0
for
N
large enough, there exists a sequence
\{c_{i}\}
with
c_{i}\in k
such that
Eq.

3.7
holds and
c_{M}=0
for
M\geq N+1
. We do induction on
N
. For the base case
N=1
, then we take
c_{1}=u_{1}^{-1}b_{0}
and
c_{i}=0
for
i>1
, which already guarantees that
Eq.

3.7
holds. Suppose the statement holds up to
N
for
N\geq 1
and we are given a sequence
\{b_{i}\}
with
b_{i}=0
for
i\geq N+1
. We let
c_{N+1}=u_{1}^{-1}b_{N}
and
c_{j}=0
for
j\geq N+2
. Apply the induction to the sequence
\{b_{i}^{\prime}\}_{0\leq i\leq N-1}
with
b_{i}^{\prime}=b_{i}
if
c_{N+1}
doesn’t show up on the right handside of
Eq.

3.7
, otherwise
b_{i}^{\prime}=b_{i}-c_{N+1}u_{N+1-i}
, we get a sequence
\{c_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq N}
solving
Eq.

3.7
for
b_{i}^{\prime}
(
0\leq i\leq N-1
), then by construction
\{c_{j}\}_{1\leq j\leq N+1}
solving
Eq.

3.7
for
b_{i}
(
0\leq i\leq N+1
), we win.
∎

Similar to
[
BL22a
, Propostition 3.5.11]
and
[
AHB22
, Proposition 2.7]
, we can draw the following consequence.

Proposition 3.2.3
.

Assume that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
, the natural morphism
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\mathcal{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
constructed in
Proposition

3.1.23
is a fiber sequence.

Proof.

First we consider
\mathcal{E}=\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
. By the discussion at the beginning of this subsection,
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
descends to a functor from
\mathcal{E}
to itself and hence we have a morphism

\mathcal{O}_{X^{\operatorname{HT}}}\to\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{\partial}\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X},

which is a fiber sequence by
Lemma

3.2.2
and faithfully flat descent. For a general
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
, tensoring
\mathcal{E}
with the above sequence and then applying the projection formula yields a fiber sequence

\mathcal{E}\to\rho_{*}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\partial^{\prime}}\rho_{*}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}

After taking cohomology we then get a fiber sequence

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial^{\prime}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}

Then one can check that
\partial^{\prime}
is precisely
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
by the usual trick of trivializing Hopf algebra’s comodules.
∎

Example 3.2.4

(
q
-connections on the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}^{k}
when restricted to the Hodge-Tate locus)
.

For
\mathscr{E}=\mathcal{I}^{k}
, one can verify that under the trivialization
\rho^{*}\mathscr{E}=\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot(d^{k})
, the
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-linear
\partial_{\mathscr{E}}
is given by multiplication by
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\binom{k}{i}e^{i}\beta^{i-1}=e\cdot\frac{(1+p^{\alpha+1})^{k}-1}{p^{\alpha+1}}
, this is due to the fact that
\psi(d^{k})=(1+\epsilon e)^{k}d^{k}
, and

(1+\epsilon e)^{k}-1=\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{k}\binom{k}{i}e^{i}\beta^{i-1}=\epsilon\frac{(1+e\beta)^{k}-1}{\beta}=\epsilon\frac{(1+p^{\alpha+1})^{k}-1}{\beta}=\epsilon e\cdot\frac{(1+p^{\alpha+1})^{k}-1}{p^{\alpha+1}}.

Here we use the fact that
p^{\alpha+1}=e\beta
obtained in
Lemma

3.2.1
.

Remark 3.2.5
.

Proposition

3.2.3
fails when
p=2
and
\alpha=0
. Actually, in this case
e=1
and
\beta=2
, then if it holds, the previous example implies that
\mathrm{H}^{1}(X^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{I}^{2})=\mathbb{Z}/4
, a contradiction with the fact that
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{I}^{2})=\mathbb{Z}/2
by
[
BL22a
, Proposition 3.5.11, Corollary 3.5.14]
.

3.3.
q
-connections beyond the Hodge-Tate locus

With all of the ingredients in hand, in this subsection, we classify quasi-coherent complexes on
X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

Assume that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the natural morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\mathcal{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}

constructed in
Proposition

3.1.23
is a fiber sequence.

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, to see it induces an identification of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})
with
\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Theta_{\mathscr{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})
, using standard dévissage (the trick used in the proof of
[
Liu24
, Proposition 3.15]
), by induction on
n
, it reduces to
n=1
, which follows from
Proposition

3.2.3
.

Finally for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, as taking global sections commutes with limits, by writing
\mathcal{E}
as the inverse limit of
\mathcal{E}_{n}
for
\mathcal{E}_{n}
the restriction of
\mathcal{E}
to
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n}
, we see that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})=\varprojlim\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n},\mathcal{E}_{n})=\varprojlim\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\mathscr{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{n})=\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\mathcal{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}).

Here the second equality follows from the first paragraph and the last equality holds as finite limits commute with limits.
∎

As a byproduct of
Proposition

3.3.1
, we conclude that

Corollary 3.3.2
.

Assume that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. The global sections functor

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\bullet):\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\quad\text{resp.}\quad\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n},\bullet):\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})

commutes with colimits.

Proposition 3.3.3
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
(resp.
\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n})
) is generated under shifts and colimits by the invertible sheaves
\mathcal{I}^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Arguing as in
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.5.16]
, this could be reduced to
n=1
, where the results follow from
[
AHB22
, Proposition 2.9]
.
∎

Theorem 3.3.4
.

Assume that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The functor

\displaystyle\beta_{n}^{+}:\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]),\qquad\mathcal{E}\mapsto(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E}),\partial_{\mathcal{E}})

constructed in
Proposition

3.1.21
is fully faithful. Moreover, its essential image consists of those objects
M\in\mathcal{D}(A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])
satisfying the following pair of conditions:

•

M
is
(p,d)
-complete.

•

The action of
\partial
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}k)

15
15

15

Here the derived tensor product means the derived base change along
A/d^{n}\to A/(d,q-1)=k
.
is locally nilpotent.

Proof.

The functor is well-defined thanks to
Proposition

3.1.21
. Then we follow the proof of
[
BL22a
, Theorem 3.5.8]
.
For the full faithfulness, let
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
be quasi-coherent complexes on
X^{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{n}
and we want to show that the natural map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(X^{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{n})}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])}(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E}),\rho^{*}(\mathcal{F}))

is a homotopy equivalence. Thanks to
Proposition

3.3.3
, we could reduce to the case that
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{I}^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Replacing
\mathcal{F}
by the twist
\mathcal{F}(-k)
, we could further assume that
k=0
. Then the desired result follows from
Remark

3.1.16
and
Proposition

3.3.1
.

To check that the action of
\partial
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}k)
is locally nilpotent for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, again thanks to
Proposition

3.3.3
, we might assume
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{I}^{r}
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then by
Example

3.2.4
, after base change to
k
the action of
\partial
is given by multiplication by
re
,
which already vanishes as our assumption (
p>2
or
\alpha>0
) guarantees that
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is ramified over
W(k)
, hence
e=0
in
k
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])
be the full subcategory spanned by objects satisfying two conditions listed in
Theorem

3.3.4
. As the source
\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by the invertible sheaves
\mathcal{I}^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by
Proposition

3.3.3
, to complete the proof it suffices to show that
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is also generated under shifts and colimits by
\{\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{k})\}
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}
). For this purpose, it can be reduced to the Hodge-Tate locus. Indeed, if
\mathcal{E}
is in
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}(\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{k}),\mathcal{E})\cong\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}(\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{O}),\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{-k})\otimes\mathcal{E})=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
16
16

16

Here the underlying complex of
\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{-k})\otimes\mathcal{E}
is
\rho^{*}(\mathcal{I}^{-k})\otimes_{A/d^{n}}\mathcal{E}
and
\partial
acts on it via the twisted Leibnitz rule stated in
Remark

3.1.13
.
, then we wish to show that
\mathcal{E}=0
, which is equivalent to the vanishing of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}:=\mathcal{E}/^{\mathbb{L}}d
due to the
(p,d)
-completeness assumption on
\mathcal{E}
.

By considering the fiber sequence

\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}(\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{O}),\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{1-k})\otimes\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{n}}(\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{O}),\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{-k})\otimes\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\beta_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{O}),(\mathcal{I}^{-k}\mathcal{E})|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}),

we deduce that
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\beta_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{k}),\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}})
vanishes for all
k
. We will show that this already guarantees the vanishing of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
.

Indeed, we argue that for every nonzero object
\mathcal{E}_{0}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\beta_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{E}_{0})\neq 0
. For this purpose, first we observe that
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\beta_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{E}_{0})\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\mathcal{E}_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathcal{E}_{0})
by
Remark

3.1.16
. Replacing
\mathcal{E}_{0}
by
\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes k
(the derived Nakayama guarantees that
L\otimes k
detects whether
L
is zero or not for
p
-complete
L
), we may assume that there exists some cohomology group
\mathrm{H}^{-m}(\mathcal{E}_{0})
containing a nonzero element killed by
\partial_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}
(this could be done by iterating the action of
\partial
and then use the nilpotence assumption). It then follows that there exists a non-zero morphism from
\beta_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{O}[m])
to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, we win.
∎

When restricting the above theorem to perfect complexes, we get the following result.

Corollary 3.3.5
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.. The functor
\beta^{+}_{n}
from
Theorem

3.3.4
restricts to a fully faithful functor

\beta^{+}_{n}\colon\mathrm{Perf}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\to\mathrm{Perf}(A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])

whose essential image consists of
(p,d)
-adically complete perfect complexes
M
over
A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]
for which
\partial
is nilpotent on
H^{\ast}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A/d^{n}}k)
. Moreover,
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{Perf}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
comes from a vector bundle on
X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
if and only if
\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{E})
is further required to be a finite projective
A/d^{n}
-module concentrated on degree
0
.

Proof.

Given
\mathcal{E}\in\mathrm{Perf}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the underlying complex of
\beta^{+}_{n}(\mathcal{E})
is
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
, hence it is a
(p,d)
-adically complete perfect complex over
A/d^{n}
. To see it is perfect over
A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]
, by (non-commutative) derived Nakayama, it suffices to show that
\beta^{+}_{n}(\mathcal{E})\otimes_{A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]}^{\mathbb{L}}A/d[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]\simeq\beta^{+}_{1}(\mathcal{E}|_{X^{\operatorname{HT}}})
is perfect over
A/d[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]=\mathcal{O}_{K}[\partial]
, which follows from its perfectness over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
and the regularity of
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\partial]
. Consequently
\beta^{+}_{n}
in
Theorem

3.3.4
does restrict to a functor stated in the corollary and its full faithfulness follows from
Theorem

3.3.4
.

Conversely, given a
(p,d)
-complete
M\in\mathrm{Perf}(A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])
such that
\partial
is nilpotent on
H^{\ast}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A/d^{n}}k)
, we need to show that
M
is a perfect
A/d^{n}
-complex. As
A/d^{n}
is complete with respect to the
(p,d)
-topology, it suffices to check this for
M\otimes_{A/d^{n}}k
. By further noticing that canonical truncations of
M
are again perfect
A/d^{n}[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]
complexes, we reduce to the case that
M
is concentrated on degree
0
and lives over
k
. Then
M
is a finitely generated
k[\partial]/\partial^{i}
-module for some
i\geq 0
, hence a perfect
A/d^{n}
-module, as desired.

For the moreover part, one just observes that
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
if and only if
\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{E})
is concentrated on degree
0
by
Proposition

3.1.18
.
∎

3.4.
Comparison with the
q
-de Rham prism

In this section we would like to present
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
as a quotient of the affine formal scheme
\mathrm{Spf}(A)
, motivated by the construction in
[
BL22a
, Section 3.8]
as well as the following consequence of
Proposition

3.1.9
, namely we can consider the restriction of
\gamma_{b,c}
to the locus
\epsilon=q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)
and thus obtain the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon=q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{b,c}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},}

where
\gamma_{A}
is the atuomorphism of
A
sending
q
to
q^{1+p^{\alpha+1}}
. Briefly speaking, the diagram provides an isomorphism between
\rho\circ\gamma_{A}
and
\rho
, viewed as points in
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(A)
.

We next upgrade the above diagram to show that
\rho
factors through a quotient of
\mathrm{Spf}(A)
. First we observe that the
q
-prism
(A,I)=(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}})
admits an action by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
, where an element
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
acts by the automorphism
q\mapsto q^{u}
, which will be denoted as
\gamma_{u}
later. Indeed, one could check that for
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
,
\gamma_{u}
preserves the ideal
(d)
if and only if
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.

Proposition 3.4.1
.

\rho\circ\gamma_{u}\in X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(A)
is isomorphic to
\rho\in X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(A)
if and only if
u\in(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
. Moreover, the above
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
-action induces a morphism of stacks

[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\to X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},

where
[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]
is the stack quotient of
\mathrm{Spf}(A)
by the profinite group
(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
.

Proof.

Following the strategy of proving
Proposition

3.1.9
, specifying an isomorphism between
\rho\circ\gamma_{u}
and
\rho
is equivalent to giving the following data:

•

An element
b_{u}\in W(A)
(it will be automatically in
W(A)^{\times}
if exists) such that
\tilde{\gamma_{u}}(d)=\tilde{\iota}(d)\cdot b_{u}
.

•

An element
c_{u}\in W(A)
such that
\tilde{\gamma_{u}}(q)-\tilde{\iota}(q)=\tilde{\iota}(d)\cdot c_{u}
.

Here in above
\tilde{\gamma_{u}}:A\to W(A)
(resp.
\tilde{\iota}:A\to W(A)
) is the unique
\delta
-ring morphism lifting
\gamma_{u}:A\to A
(resp.
\iota=\mathrm{Id}:A\to A
).

One can check that such a
b_{u}
exists if and only if
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
by mimicking the proof of
Proposition

3.1.5
. We leave it as an exercise for the reader. Heuristically, this is due to the fact that
\gamma_{u}
preserves the ideal
(d)
and
\frac{\gamma_{u}(d)}{d}\in A^{\times}
for
u\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{\times}
.

For the existence of
c_{u}
, we proceed as in the proof of
Proposition

3.1.8
. First notice that
w_{n}(\tilde{\iota}(q))=w_{0}(\varphi^{n}(\tilde{\iota}(q)))=w_{0}(\tilde{\iota}(\varphi^{n}(q)))=q^{p^{n}}
and that
w_{n}(\tilde{\gamma_{u}}(q))=q^{up^{n}}
,
w_{n}(d)=[p]_{q^{p^{n+\alpha}}}
. Hence there exists
r_{n}\in A
such that
w_{n}(\tilde{\gamma_{u}}(q))-w_{n}(\tilde{\iota}(q))=w_{n}(d)\cdot r_{n}
if and only if
[p]_{q^{p^{n+\alpha}}}|~q^{up^{n}}-q^{p^{n}}
, which is equivalent to that
u\equiv 1\mod p^{\alpha+1}
. Moreover, if
u\in(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
, then we define
r_{n}
to be
\frac{q^{up^{n}}-q^{p^{n}}}{[p]_{q^{p^{n+\alpha}}}}
and observe that
\varphi(r_{n})=r_{n+1}
. By invoking Dwork’s lemma (see
[
Laz06
, 4.6 on page 213]
for details), we conclude that
(r_{0},r_{1},\cdots)\in R^{\mathbb{N}}
is the ghost coordinate for some element
c_{u}\in W(A)
(such
c_{u}
is unique as the ghost map is injective). By construction,
c_{u}
satisfies that
\tilde{\gamma_{u}}(q)-\tilde{\iota}(q)=\tilde{\iota}(d)\cdot c_{u}
, hence finish the proof of the first part in the proposition.

For the moreover part, we just observe that for
u\in(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
, the isomorphism between
\rho\circ\gamma_{u}
and
\rho
is unique as both
b_{u}
and
c_{u}
are unique (as
W(A)
is
\tilde{\iota}(d)
-torsion free, which can be checked after applying ghost map), hence the isomorphism
\sigma_{u}:\rho\circ\gamma_{u}\simeq\rho
satisfies the “higher associativity" condition in
[
Rom05
, Definition 1.3]
, hence
\rho
factors through
[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\to X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
.
∎

Remark 3.4.2
.

Unfortunately, the above morphism
[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\to X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
is not an isomorphism. However, as
\alpha
increases, it becomes closer to an isomorphism heuristically. Indeed, by varying
\alpha
and considering the inverse limit of the following commutative diagram (which corresponds to perfection)

\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{2}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{q\mapsto q^{p}}
\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(W(k)[\zeta_{p^{2}}]_{p})^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(W(k)[\zeta_{p}]_{p})^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},}

we finally end with an isomorphism
\mathrm{Spf}({A_{\mathrm{inf}}}(W(k)[\zeta_{p^{\infty}}]_{p}^{\wedge}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\rightarrow}}(W(k)[\zeta_{p^{\infty}}]_{p}^{\wedge})^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
.

In what follows, we will abuse notation by still writing the morphism
[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\to X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
as
\rho
.

For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, each element
u\in(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
determines an automorphism of
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
, which we will denote by
\gamma_{u}
. For
u=1+p^{\alpha+1}
(which is a topological generator of
(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
), the automorphism
\gamma_{u}
is completely determined by the
q
-connection on
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
.

Proposition 3.4.3
.

For
u_{0}=1+p^{\alpha+1}
,
\gamma_{u_{0}}
acts on
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
via
1+q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

This follows from our construction of
\gamma_{u}
,
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
and the commutative diagram given above
Proposition

3.4.1
.
∎

Remark 3.4.4
.

As
q
is a unit and
u_{0}
is a topologically generator for
(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
, combining
Proposition

3.4.3
with
Theorem

3.3.4
, we see that for
\mathcal{E}\in X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
,

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{\mathcal{E}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})=L\eta_{q^{p^{\alpha}}-1}\mathrm{R}\Gamma([\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}],\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}),

where
L\eta
is the décalage functor (see
[
BMS18
]
or
[
BO15
]
for details).
In particular, pullback from
\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
to
\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}])
along

\rho:[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\to X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
is not fully faithful.

Remark 3.4.5

(Comparing the
q
-connection with Sen operator on the Hodge-Tate locus)
.

We can restrict
\rho
to the Hodge-Tate locus and obtain
\rho:[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\to X^{\operatorname{HT}}
. Consequently, for
u\in(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
and
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
,
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
is equipped with an automorphism
\gamma_{u}
. On the other hand,
\mathcal{E}
is equipped with two operators: the
q
-connection
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
from
Theorem

3.3.4
(constructed via the
q
prism) as well as the Sen operator
\theta_{\mathcal{E}}
from
[
AHB22
, Theorem 2.5]
(constructed utilizing the Breuil-Kisin prism), hence so is
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
. The relations between the action of
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\theta_{\mathcal{E}}
on

\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
can be connected via the automorphism
\gamma_{u_{0}}
. Indeed, one can copy the proof of
[
BL22a
, Proposition 3.7.1]
to conclude that for
u\in(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
,
\gamma_{u}
acts on
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
via
u^{\theta_{\mathcal{E}}/E^{\prime}(\pi)}
, but we already know that
\gamma_{u_{0}}
acts on
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
via
1+q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
thanks to
Proposition

3.4.3
. Hence

(1+p^{\alpha+1})^{\theta_{\mathcal{E}}/E^{\prime}(\pi)}=1+q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\partial_{\mathcal{E}}=1+\frac{p^{\alpha+1}}{e}\partial_{\mathcal{E}},

where the last identity is due to
Lemma

3.2.1
. As an upsot, we see

\partial_{\mathcal{E}}=e\cdot\frac{(1+p^{\alpha+1})^{\theta_{\mathcal{E}}/E^{\prime}(\pi)}-1}{p^{\alpha+1}}.

In this sense,
Example

3.2.4
is just a special case of the above formula!

By examining the construction of the isomorphism
\sigma_{u}:\rho\circ\gamma_{u}\simeq\rho
, we can determine the locus where
\sigma_{u}
is trivial very explicitly.

Proposition 3.4.6
.

We have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{p^{\alpha}}\mapsto 1}
\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}.}

Proof.

We observe that the action of
(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
on
(A,d)
is trivial after modulo
q^{p^{\alpha}}-1
. Moreover, for
u\in(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
, the isomorphism
\sigma_{u}:\rho\circ\gamma_{u}\simeq\rho
constructed in
Proposition

3.4.1
becomes the identify on the vanishing locus of
q^{p^{\alpha}}-1
, which is implied by the vanishing of
r_{n}
constructed in
Proposition

3.4.1
on
A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)
(indeed, this follows from the vanishing of
r_{0}=q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)
). ∎

Then the main result of this section is the following theorem:

Theorem 3.4.7
.

Let
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. Then the diagram in
Proposition

3.4.6
induces a fully faithful functor of
\infty
-categories

F:\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}])\times_{\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}])}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))).

The essential image of
F
consists of those pairs
(M,\gamma_{M})
with
M\in\mathcal{D}(A)
and
\gamma_{M}
being an automorphism of
M
which is congruent to the identity modulo
q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)
such that the following holds:

•

M\in\mathcal{D}(A)
is
(p,d)
-complete.

•

the action of
\partial_{M}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}k)

17
17

17

Here the derived tensor product means the derived base change along
A/d^{n}\to A/(d,q-1)=k
.
is locally nilpotent, where
\partial_{M}:M\to M
is an operator satisfying
\gamma_{M}=\mathrm{Id}+q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\partial_{M}
.

In particular, for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the diagram

(3.8)

\begin{gathered}\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 107.0124pt\raise-33.52866pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.07016pt\raise-33.52866pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 120.07016pt\raise-33.52866pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma([\mathrm{Spf}(A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}],\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{gathered}

is a pullback square in the
\infty
-category
\hat{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
.

Proof.

We first prove full faithfulness. We use
\mathcal{C}
to denote the fiber product

\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}])\times_{\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}])}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))).

Let
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
be quasi-coherent complexes on
X^{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}
. We aim to show that the natural map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(X^{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}})}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(F(\mathcal{E}),F(\mathcal{G}))

is a homotopy equivalence. By
Proposition

3.3.3
,
\mathcal{D}(X^{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by
\mathcal{I}^{n}
, we could reduce to the case that
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{I}^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Replacing
\mathcal{G}
by the twist
\mathcal{G}(-k)
, we could further assume that
k=0
. Then it suffices to show that
Eq.

3.8
is indeed a pullback suare for any

\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
. Let
u=1+p^{\alpha+1}
be the topological generator for
(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}
. Then

\mathrm{R}\Gamma([\mathrm{Spf}(A)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}],\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})=\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{u}-1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}),

for which we will denote as
(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})^{u=1}
for simplicity. Consequently
Eq.

3.8
can be rewritten as a diagram of complexes

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})^{u=1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})^{u=1}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1).}

By
Proposition

3.4.3
,

(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})^{u=1}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)=((\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))\oplus((\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))[-1]

and hence the cofiber of the bottom map
(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\to(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})^{u=1}
is precisely
((\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))[-1]
. Thanks to
Proposition

3.3.1
and
Proposition

3.4.3
, we obtain a commutative diagram in which all of the columns are fiber sequences

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})^{u=1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{((\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{\mathcal{E}}}
\textstyle{\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{u}-\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cdot q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)}
\textstyle{\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}_{A}A/(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1),}

hence the first row is also a fiber sequence, we thus finish the proof of full faithfulness.

For the essential image of the functor
F
, first we observe that an object
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{C}
can be identified with a pair
(M,\gamma_{M})
where
M\in\mathcal{D}(A)
and
\gamma_{M}
is an automorphism of
M
congruent to the identity modulo
q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)
.

Given
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, to check that the action of
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E})\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}k)
is locally nilpotent, again thanks to
Proposition

3.3.3
, we might assume
\mathcal{E}=(\mathcal{I})^{r}
for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then by
Example

3.2.4
, after base change to
k
the action of
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
is given by multiplication by
re
,
which already vanishes as our assumption (
p>2
or
\alpha>0
) guarantees that
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is ramified over
W(k)
, hence
e=0
in
k
.

Let
\mathcal{J}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be the full subcategory spanned by objects satisfying two conditions listed in
Theorem

3.3.4
. As the source
\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by the invertible sheaves
\mathcal{I}^{n}
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}
by
Proposition

3.3.3
, to complete the proof it suffices to show that
\mathcal{J}
is also generated under shifts and colimits by
\{F(\mathcal{I}^{k})\}
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}
). For this purpose, it can be reduced to the Hodge-Tate locus. Indeed, if
\mathcal{E}
is in
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}}(F(\mathcal{I}^{k}),\mathcal{E})\cong\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}}(F(\mathcal{O}),F(\mathcal{I}^{-k})\otimes\mathcal{E})=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, then we wish to show that
\mathcal{E}=0
, which is equivalent to the vanishing of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}}
due to the
(p,d)
-completeness assumption on
\mathcal{E}
. Here we denote
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}
as the fiber product

\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A/d)/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}])\times_{\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A/(d,q^{p^{\alpha}}-1))/(1+p^{\alpha+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p})^{\times}])}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(A/(d,q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)))

and there is a natural restriction functor from
\mathcal{C}
to
\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}
, under which
\mathcal{E}
is sent to
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}}
.

By considering the fiber sequence

\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}}(F(\mathcal{O}),F(\mathcal{I}^{1-k})\otimes\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}}(F(\mathcal{O}),F(\mathcal{I}^{-k})\otimes\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}}(F(\mathcal{O})|_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}},(\mathcal{I}^{-k}\mathcal{E})|_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}}),

we deduce that
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}}(F(\mathcal{I}^{k})|_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}},\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}})
vanishes for all
k
. We will show that this already guarantees the vanishing of
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}}
.

Indeed, we argue that for every nonzero object
\mathcal{E}_{0}=(M,\gamma_{M})\in\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}
(here
M\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
,
\gamma_{M}=\mathrm{Id}+q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)\partial_{M}
is an automorphism of
M
which is congruent to the identity modulo
q(q^{p^{\alpha}}-1)
) which further satisfies that
M
is
p
-complete and that the action of
\partial_{M}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}k)
is locally nilpotent,
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}}(F(\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{E}_{0})\neq 0
. For this purpose, first we observe that
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}^{\operatorname{HT}}}(F(\mathcal{O}),\mathcal{E}_{0})=\operatorname{fib}(M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M)
by repeating the proof of the full faithfulness. Replacing
M
by
M\otimes k
(the derived Nakayama guarantees that
L\otimes k
detects whether
L
is zero or not for
p
-complete
L
), we may assume that there exists some cohomology group
\mathrm{H}^{-m}(M)
containing a nonzero element killed by
\partial_{M}
(this could be done by iterating the action of
\partial
and then use the nilpotence assumption). It then follows that there exists a non-zero morphism from
F(\mathcal{O})[m])
to
\mathcal{E}_{0}
, hence we finish the proof.
∎

Remark 3.4.8
.

The above theorem still holds if we replace
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
with
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n}
and substitute
A
by
A/d^{n}
in the statement, hence we get the corresponding classification of truncated prismatic crystals on
X_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
as well. The proof is exactly the same.

3.5.
Applications to quasi-coherent complexes on the Cartier-Witt stack

In this subsection we take
\alpha=0
and consider the prism
(A,I)=(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]],[p]_{q})\in X_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
for
X=\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})=\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\zeta_{p}])
. In other words, we specialize the discussion in previous subsections to
k=\mathbb{F}_{p},\alpha=0
.

First we recollect some basics for the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-action on
A
discussed in
[
BL22a
, Section 3.8]
. Recall that
A=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
admits an action of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
, which carries each element
e\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
to the automorphism of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
given by
\gamma_{e}:q\mapsto q^{[e]}
, for which
[e]
is the Teichmuller lift of
e
. Following
[
BL22a
]
, we let
\tilde{p}\in A
denote the sum

\sum_{e\in\mathbb{F}_{p}}q^{[e]}=1+\sum_{e\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}q^{[e]},

then
\tilde{p}
is invariant under the action of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
and differs a unit from
d
by
[
BL22a
, Proposition 3.8.6]
. Consequently,
\gamma_{e}
preserves the ideal
(d)
, hence induces an automorphism
\gamma_{e}:A/d^{n}\to A/d^{n}
for any
n\geq 1
. In particular, take
n=1
, we have an automorphism
\gamma_{e}
form
\mathcal{O}_{K}
to itself.

As an upshot, we could define an
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-action on
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
as follows

\begin{split}X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(R)&\longrightarrow X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(R)\\
(\alpha:I\to W(R),\eta:\mathcal{O}_{K}\to\Cone(\alpha))&\longmapsto(\alpha,\eta\circ\gamma_{e}:\mathcal{O}_{K}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{e}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\xrightarrow{\eta}\Cone(\alpha)).\end{split}

Similarly,
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
acts on
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n}
for any
n\geq 1
.

Proposition 3.5.1
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The natural morphism
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n}\to\WCart_{n}
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant, where the group
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
acts trivially on
\WCart_{n}
. Consequently it descends to a morphism
\pi:[X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\to\WCart_{n}
.

Proof.

It follows from that the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
action on
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
fixes the Cartier-Witt divisor
\alpha
.
∎

Heuristically as the coarse moduli space for
[X/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]
is
\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
,
\WCart
could be viewed as the “course moduli space" for
[X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]
. The next result provides some evidence for such a philosophy.

Theorem 3.5.2
.

Assume that
p>2
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The pullback along
\pi
induces a fully faithful functor of
\infty
-categories

\mathcal{D}(\WCart_{n})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]).

Theorem

3.5.2
is equivalent to the following a
prior
weaker result:

Theorem 3.5.3
.

Assume that
p>2
. Let
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})
, then

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}).

Proof of
Theorem

3.5.2
from
Theorem

3.5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
be quasi-coherent complexes on
X^{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{n}
and we want to show that the natural map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\WCart_{n})}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}([X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])}(\pi^{*}(\mathcal{E}),\pi^{*}(\mathcal{F}))

is a homotopy equivalence. By
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.5.16]
,
\mathcal{D}(\WCart_{n})
is generated under colimits by
\mathcal{I}^{n}
, we could reduce to the case that
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{I}^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Replacing
\mathcal{F}
by the twist
\mathcal{F}(-k)
, we could further assume that
k=0
. Then it suffices to show that for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(\WCart_{n})
,

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\WCart_{n},\mathcal{E})=\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{n}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}).

For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, as taking global sections commutes with limits, by writing
\mathcal{E}
as the inverse limit of
\mathcal{E}_{k}
for
\mathcal{E}_{k}
the restriction of
\mathcal{E}
to
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{k}
(
k\leq n
), we are reduced to the case
n=1
, which is precisely
Theorem

3.5.3
.
∎

For the proof of
Theorem

3.5.3
, we need to develop a
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant version of the isomorphism
\gamma_{b,c}
constructed in the beginning of this section.

Proposition 3.5.4
.

The covering morphism
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(A)\to X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant, where the source and target are equipped with the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-action discussed at the beginning at this subsection. Consequently, it descends to a morphism
[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\to[X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]
, which will still be denoted as
\rho
by abuse of notation.

Proof.

By
[
Rom05
, Definition 1.3]
and the discussion above that, we need to specify a
\sigma
satisfying certain certain “higher associativity" condition such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\times\mathrm{Spf}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}\times X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}}

Given
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
, it induces an automorphism
\gamma_{u}
on
A=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
by sending
q
to
q^{[u]}
, where
[u]
is the Teichmuller lift of
u
. Moreover, one can check that
\gamma_{u}
preserves the
\delta
-structure. Then unwinding the definitions, we see that the composition of
\rho
and the top arrow corresponds to the following point in
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(A)

(\alpha^{\prime}:(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\gamma}_{u}}W(A)\to W(A),\eta^{\prime}:\Cone((d)\to A)\xrightarrow{\tilde{\gamma}_{u}}\Cone(\alpha^{\prime})).

Similarly, the composition of the bottom arrow and
\rho
-corresponds to the point

(\alpha:(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W(A)\to W(A),\eta:\Cone((d)\to A)\xrightarrow{\tilde{\iota}\circ\gamma_{u}}\Cone(\alpha)),

where
\tilde{\gamma}_{u}
(resp.
\tilde{\iota}
) is the unique lift of
\gamma_{u}:A\to A
(resp.
\mathrm{Id}:A\to A
) to a
\delta
-ring morphism
A\to W(A)
. Moreover, as
\gamma_{u}
is a
\delta
-ring homomorphism,
\tilde{\gamma}_{u}=\tilde{\iota}\circ\gamma_{u}
.

Suppose
\gamma_{u}(d)=d\cdot d_{u}
, where
d_{u}\in A^{\times}
. Then
\tilde{\gamma}_{u}(d)=\tilde{\iota}\circ\gamma_{u}(d)=\tilde{\iota}(d\cdot d_{u})=\tilde{\iota}(d)\tilde{\iota}(d_{u})
, hence we could construct an automorphism of Cartier-Witt divisors
\sigma_{u}:\alpha^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\alpha
as follows:

\textstyle{(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\gamma}_{u}}W(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(d)\otimes x\mapsto(d)\otimes\tilde{\iota}(d_{u})x}
\textstyle{W(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W(A)}

Here the left vertical map is
W(A)
-linear and the commutativity of the diagram follows from our construction of
d_{u}
.

Then one could easily see that
\sigma_{u}\circ\eta^{\prime}=\eta
as
\tilde{\gamma}_{u}=\tilde{\iota}\circ\gamma_{u}
, such a data together with
\sigma_{u}
then gives the desired
\sigma
.

We leave the reader to check that
\sigma
satisfies the “higher associativity" condition in
[
Rom05
, Definition 1.3]
.
∎

Remark 3.5.5
.

By
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.8.8]
,
A^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]
, hence it’s not hard to see that
\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]])
is the coarse moduli space for
[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]
.

Recall that the construction of the
q
-connection in the first subsection replies heavily on the natural embedding
A\to R=A[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q-1)\epsilon)
, which could be promoted to a
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant embedding.

Lemma 3.5.6
.

We can equip
R
with a
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-action by extending that on
A
and requiring that

\gamma_{u}(\epsilon)=\epsilon\cdot q^{[u]-1}\frac{q^{[u]}-1}{q-1},

then both the natural embedding
A\to R=A[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q-1)\epsilon)
and
\psi:A\to R
defined in
Lemma

3.1.1
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant.

Proof.

To show that the desired
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
action on
R
is well defined, we just need to check that
\gamma_{u}(\epsilon^{2})=\gamma_{u}(q(q-1)\epsilon)
, which is left to the reader. To see
\psi
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant, it suffices to check that
\psi(\gamma_{u}(q))=\gamma_{u}(\psi(q))
, but this follows from the following calculation using
Lemma

3.1.1
.

\begin{split}\psi(\gamma_{u}(q))&=q^{[u]}+\epsilon[p[u]]_{q}q^{[u]-1}=q^{[u]}+\epsilon\cdot q^{[u]-1}\cdot\frac{q^{[u]p}-1}{q-1},\\
\gamma_{u}(\psi(q))&=\gamma_{u}(q+\epsilon\frac{q^{p}-1}{q-1})=q^{[u]}+\epsilon\cdot q^{[u]-1}\frac{q^{[u]}-1}{q-1}\frac{q^{[u]p}-1}{q^{[u]}-1}=q^{[u]}+\epsilon\cdot q^{[u]-1}\cdot\frac{q^{[u]p}-1}{q-1}.\end{split}

∎

By mimicking the proof of
Proposition

3.5.4
, we have that the
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(R)\to X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
is also
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant. As an upshot, we can promote
Proposition

3.5.4
to a
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant diagram.

Proposition 3.5.7
.

The diagram constructed in
Proposition

3.1.9
and
Corollary

3.1.10
are both
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant. Hence it descends to a commutative diagram

\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(R)/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{b,c}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{[X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{[X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}

Proof.

Given
Proposition

3.5.4
and
Lemma

3.5.6
, all of the arrows in
Proposition

3.1.9
are
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant.
∎

Corollary 3.5.8
.

For
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])
,
\gamma_{b,c}:\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon)
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant.

Proof.

This follows from last proposition and the construction of
\gamma_{b,c}
.
∎

Now we are ready to prove
Theorem

3.5.3
.

Proof of
Theorem

3.5.3
.

Given
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})
, we wish to show that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}).

As both sides are
p
-complete, it suffices to prove that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}.

The strategy is to calculate both sides explicitly and then identify them after modulo
p
. Actually, applying
[
BL22a
, Proposition 3.5.11]
, we see that

(3.9)

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}=\operatorname{fib}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p\xrightarrow{\theta}\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p).

On the other hand, if we abuse the notation by denoting the pullback of
\mathcal{E}
to
\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
still as
\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}
, then

\begin{split}&\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{\operatorname{HT}},\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}\\
=&(\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\gamma_{b,c}-\mathrm{Id}=\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}\cdot\epsilon}\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}\cdot\epsilon))^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}\\
=&\operatorname{fib}((\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}\xrightarrow{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}\cdot\epsilon}(\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}\cdot\epsilon)^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}),\end{split}

here the second equality follows from
Proposition

3.3.1
, the last identity holds since
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
is a finite group of order relatively prime to
p
(hence no higher cohomology when taking
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
invariants on
p
-complete complexes) and
\gamma_{b,c}
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant by
Corollary

3.5.8
.

Next we calculate
(\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}\cdot\epsilon)^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}
. By
Lemma

3.5.10
, the following square is commutative up to the isomorphism we constructed in the proof of it,

\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}}.}

From now on we fix this isomorphism
\eta\circ f\simeq\pi\circ\rho
. Then

(\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}=Rf_{*}(\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})\simeq Rf_{*}(f^{*}\eta^{*}\mathcal{E})=\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}.

Similarly, considering the commutative (up to an isomorphism) diagram

\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon))/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}}
\textstyle{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}}}

leads to that

(\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{K}}\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon))^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}=Rf_{*}(\rho^{\prime*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})\simeq Rf_{*}(f^{*}\eta^{*}\mathcal{E})=\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv).

Here
\rho^{\prime}
is the composition of
[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon))/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\to[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]
and
\rho
(hence the commutativity follows from
Lemma

3.5.10
as well), and the last equality is due to the projection formula and
Lemma

3.5.11
. In particular,
v
is identified with
e\epsilon
.

In summary, we have that

(3.10)

\begin{split}\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})&\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{fib}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}\cdot\epsilon}\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\cdot v)=\operatorname{fib}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\frac{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}}{e}\cdot v}\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\cdot v),\\
\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{p}&\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{fib}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p\xrightarrow{\frac{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}}{e}\cdot v}\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{p}[v]/(v^{2})).\end{split}

Here we use
\frac{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}}{e}
to denote the monodromy operator on
\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}
itself induced by the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-invariant parts of
\gamma_{b,c}
. Comparing with
Eq.

3.9
and unwinding all of our constructions and chosen isomorphism, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\otimes\mathbb{F}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\operatorname{fib}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{\mathcal{E}}}
\textstyle{\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathbb{F}_{p}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\operatorname{fib}(\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\frac{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}}{e}}
\textstyle{\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p).}

Hence we are left to show that
\frac{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}}{e}
coincides with
\theta_{\mathcal{E}}
constructed in
[
BL22a
]
after modulo
p
. This requires a closer review of the construction of such monodromy operators. First we claim that the induced
q
-connection
\frac{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}}{e}
on
\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}
shown up in
Eq.

3.10
precisely corresponds to the monodromy operator
\partial_{v,\mathcal{E}}
induced by
1+v\in\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv))
, viewed as an element in the automorphism group of

\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}}\times_{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})}\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv).

Indeed, following the discussion above
Lemma

3.2.1
, we could identify
\gamma_{b,c}
with
(1+e\epsilon,\epsilon)\in G_{\pi}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q-1)\epsilon))
. Hence under the natural projection
G_{\pi}\to\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}
,
\gamma_{b,c}\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q-1)\epsilon))
(by abuse of notation) precisely corresponds to the restriction of
1+v\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv))
via the structure morphism
\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-q(q-1)\epsilon))\to\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv))
sending
v
to
e\epsilon
. This implies that under our fixed isomorphism
\eta\circ f\simeq\pi\circ\rho
,
\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}:\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}\to\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}
is identified with

\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}\simeq\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\xrightarrow{\partial_{v,\mathcal{E}}\otimes e\cdot\mathrm{Id}}\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\simeq\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E},

hence
\frac{\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}}{e}=\partial_{v,\mathcal{E}}
on
\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}
.

Finally we are left to compare
\partial_{v}
and
\theta
after modulo
p
. A key observation is that after modulo
p
,
\gamma=1+v\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[v]/v^{2})
and all higher divided powers of
v
vanish, which crucially uses the assumption that
p>2
to guarantee that
\frac{v^{n}}{n!}=\frac{p^{n-1}v}{n!}
is divisible by
p
for all
n>1
. However, as
\theta
on
\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}
in
[
BL22a
]
is actually constructed via
1+[v]\in W[F](\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/v^{2})
, which corresponds to
1+v\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/v^{2})
(with higher divided powers of
v
vanished) and hence exactly reduces to our
\gamma
after modulo
p
, we conclude that
\partial_{v}=\theta
on
\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}/p
, hence finish the proof.
∎

The above proof implicitly tells us we could define a
q
-Higgs connection on
\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}
for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})
, which we summarize as the following proposition since it will be used in the proof of
Theorem

3.5.15
later.

Proposition 3.5.9
.

The chosen element
1+v\in\mathbb{G}_{m}^{\sharp}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv))
induces an endomorphism
\partial_{v,\mathcal{E}}:\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\to\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}
for any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})
such that
\partial_{\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}}=\partial_{v,\mathcal{E}}\otimes e
under the isomorphism
\rho^{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{E}\simeq\eta^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{K}
induced by the chosen isomorphism in the proof of
Lemma

3.5.10
.
. Moreover, pullback along
\eta:\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}}
induces a fully faithful functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\partial]),\qquad\mathcal{E}\mapsto(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E}),\partial_{v,\mathcal{E}}),

with the essential image consisting of those
M\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[\partial])
such that
M
is
p
-complete and that
\partial^{p}-\partial
acts on
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{F}_{p})
in a locally nilpotent way.

Proof.

The statement before the moreover part and the full faithfulness in the moreover part is already given in the proof of
Theorem

3.5.3
. For the essential image, following the proof of
Theorem

3.3.4
, we just need to check the given nilpotence condition holds for
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{I}^{k}
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}
), which can be checked easily using
Example

3.5.12
.
∎

The following several lemmas are used in the proof of
Theorem

3.5.3
.

Lemma 3.5.10
.

The following square commutes up to a (non-unique) isomorphism.

\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}}}

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to\WCart
(which is actually
\pi\circ\rho
, we abuse notation here) is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\eta:\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to\WCart
. By construction
\rho(\mathcal{O}_{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}}\mathcal{O}_{K})
corresponds to the Cartier-Witt divisor

\alpha:(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to W(\mathcal{O}_{K}),

where
\tilde{\iota}:A\to W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
is the unique
\delta
-ring homomorphism lifting
A\to\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

On the other hand,
\eta(\mathcal{O}_{K}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}}\mathcal{O}_{K})
corresponds to the Cartier-Witt divisor

\alpha^{\prime}:W(\mathcal{O}_{K})\xrightarrow{V(1)}W(\mathcal{O}_{K}).

Notice that
\tilde{\iota}(d)=\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}[q^{i}]
is mapped to
0
under the projection map
W(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to\mathcal{O}_{K}
, hence
\tilde{\iota}(d)=V(x)
for some
x\in W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
. As
px=F(V(x))=\sum_{i=0}^{p-1}[q^{ip}]=p
in
W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
,
x
much be
1
since
W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
is
p
-torsion free. The following diagram

\textstyle{(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W(\mathcal{O}_{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda:d\mapsto V(1)}
\textstyle{(V(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{W(\mathcal{O}_{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{W(\mathcal{O}_{K})}

then gives an isomorphism between
\rho
and
\eta
.

Moreover, one can check that
\iota(\gamma_{u}(d))=V(1)=\gamma_{u}(V(1))
, hence the isomorphism we constructed above is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant, hence finish the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.5.11
.

Let
f:[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon))/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}
be the structure morphism, then

Rf_{*}\mathcal{O}\simeq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv),

for which
v
corresponds to
e\epsilon
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
is a finite group of order relatively prime to
p
,
\mathrm{H}^{i}(\mathcal{O}_{K},\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times})=0
for
i>0
, it then suffices to check that the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-invariants in
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon)
are given by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv)
with
v=e\epsilon
. By our notation,
e=\frac{p}{q(q-1)}\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
(as usual
q
means
\zeta_{p}
in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
). Then
\forall u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
, as
\gamma_{u}(\epsilon)=\epsilon\cdot q^{[u]-1}\frac{q^{[u]}-1}{q-1}
by definition, we see that

\gamma_{u}(e\epsilon)=\frac{p}{q^{[u]}(q^{[u]}-1)}\cdot\epsilon\cdot q^{[u]-1}\frac{q^{[u]}-1}{q-1}=\epsilon\frac{p}{q(q-1)}=e\epsilon.

Hence
\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cdot e\epsilon\subseteq(\mathcal{O}_{K}/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon))^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}
. On the other hand, if
\epsilon x
is fixed by the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
action, then an easy calculation shows that
\gamma_{u}(xq(q-1))=xq(q-1)
, hence
xq(q-1)\in\mathcal{O}_{K}^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, but
x\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
, we see that
xq(q-1)
must lives in
p\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, hence
\epsilon x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}e\epsilon
. Let
v=e\epsilon
, then
v^{2}=e^{2}\epsilon^{2}=e^{2}\beta\epsilon=pe\epsilon=pv
as
p=\beta e
.
This implies that the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-invariants in
\mathcal{O}_{K}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-\beta\epsilon)
are given by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[v]/(v^{2}-pv)
with
v=e\epsilon
.
∎

Example 3.5.12

(Explicit calculation for the ideal sheaf)
.

For
\mathscr{E}=\mathcal{I}^{k}\in\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})
, by
Example

3.2.4
and
Proposition

3.2.3
, we have that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{\operatorname{HT}},\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})=\operatorname{fib}(\mathcal{O}_{K}\xrightarrow{\cdot e\epsilon\frac{(1+p)^{k}-1}{p}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\epsilon).

After passing to
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-invariants and invoking
Lemma

3.5.11
, we get that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})=\operatorname{fib}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}\xrightarrow{\cdot v\frac{(1+p)^{k}-1}{p}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}\cdot v).

We explain why this directly implies that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{E})\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}\xrightarrow{\cdot k}\mathbb{Z}_{p})
when
p>2
. Actually, as
\frac{(1+p)^{k}-1}{p}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\binom{k}{i}p^{i-1}
, it suffices to prove that
i+v_{p}(\binom{k}{i})>1+v_{p}(k)
for
i>1
under the assumption that
p>2
. If
v_{p}(k)=0
, the statement is trivial. Otherwise let
v_{p}(k)=n>0
. Denote
v_{p}(i)=t
for a fixed
i>1
. We separate into two cases. If
t<n
, then

i+v_{p}(\binom{k}{i})=i+\frac{s(i)+s(n-i)-s(k)}{p-1}\geq i+\frac{(p-1)(n-t)}{p-1}=n+i-t\geq n+\max(p^{t},i)-t>n+1,

here the last inequality holds as
p>2
.

On the other hand, if
t\geq n
, then
i\geq p^{t}\geq p^{n}
, hence
i+v_{p}(\binom{k}{i})\geq i\geq p^{n}>1+n
. Hence we finish the proof.

Remark 3.5.13
.

Theorem

3.5.3
and
Theorem

3.5.2
fails when
p=2
. Indeed, by the previous example,
\mathrm{H}^{1}([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\pi^{*}\mathcal{I}^{2})=\mathbb{Z}/4
while
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{I}^{2})=\mathbb{Z}/2
by
[
BL22a
, Proposition 3.5.11, Corollary 3.5.14]
.

As an application of
Theorem

3.5.2
, we calculate the cohomology of the structure sheaf on the prismatic site of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

Proposition 3.5.14
.

Assume that
p>2
. The Cartier-Witt stack
\WCart
is of cohomological dimension
1
. Moreover,

\mathrm{H}^{0}(\WCart,\mathcal{O})=\mathbb{Z}_{p},~~~\quad\mathrm{H}^{1}(\WCart,\mathcal{O})\simeq\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N},n\not\equiv p\mod p+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}.

Proof.

The first sentence follows from
Theorem

3.3.4
and
Theorem

3.5.2
as there is no higher cohomology when taking
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
invariants on
p
-complete complexes. Moreover, the proof of
Theorem

3.5.2
provides us with a concrete complex to calculate
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\WCart,\mathcal{O})
, given by

(3.11)

[(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]])^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}\xrightarrow{\partial}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]\cdot\epsilon)^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}],

here the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
action on
\epsilon
is given in
Lemma

3.5.6
. First we would like to write the target of
\partial
above more explicitly. Notice that there is an injective morphism
r:(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]\cdot\epsilon)^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}\to\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
sending
a\epsilon
to
aq(q-1)
. Moreover, given the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
action on
\epsilon
regulated in
Lemma

3.5.6
, it is easy to see that the image of
r
lands in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]=(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]])^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}
, where the identity is guaranteed by
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.8.8]
.

We claim that the image of
r
consists exactly of those
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]
such that
g(p)=0
. Indeed, if
