g(\tilde{p})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]
can be factored as
q(q-1)h
in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
, then it is sent to
0
under the projection ring homomorphism
\mathrm{pr}:\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]\to\mathbb{Z}_{p},\quad,q\mapsto 1
. But
\mathrm{pr}(\tilde{p})=p
, hence
g(p)=0
. On the other hand, if
g(\tilde{p})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]
satisfies that
g(p)=0
, then viewing
g
as an element in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
, we have that
\mathrm{pr}(g)=0
. By the Weierstrass preparation theorem for formal power series,
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
can be factored as
(q-1)h
for some
h\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
. As
q
is a unit in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
,
g=q(q-1)(q^{-1}h)
is a factorization in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]
, hence we finish the proof of the claim.

Let
B\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]
be the subring consisting of those
g\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]
such that
g(p)=0
. Then the above argument shows that
r
induces an isomorphism (of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-modules) from
(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]]\cdot\epsilon)^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}
to
B
. Moreover, note that
B
could be identified with
\prod_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathbb{Z}_{p}
by sending
(a_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
in the latter to
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a_{i}e_{i}\in B
with
e_{i}:=\tilde{p}^{i}(\tilde{p}-p)
,
i\geq 0
.

Let
f=r\circ\partial:\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]\to B
, then applying
Lemma

3.1.11
for
\alpha=0
and unwinding our definition of
r
, it is easy to check that
f
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear morphism satisfying that
f(xy)=f(x)y+xf(y)+f(x)f(y)
.
As
\tilde{p}=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}}q^{[u]}=1+\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}q^{[u]}
by definition, applying
Lemma

3.1.1
and we see that

f(\tilde{p})=q(q-1)(\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}q^{[u]-1}\frac{q^{p[u]}-1}{q-1})=\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}q^{[u](p+1)}-\sum_{u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}q^{[u]},

By considering the degree and the constant term, this implies that there exists
a_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
for
1\leq i\leq p-1
such that
f(\tilde{p})=e_{p}+\sum_{i=1}^{p-1}(a_{i}e_{i})
. In general, we claim that

(3.12)

(\ast)\quad f(\tilde{p}^{k})=e_{k(p+1)+p}+\clubsuit_{k}\quad\text{with}\quad\clubsuit_{k}\in\oplus_{l=0}^{k(p+1)+p-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}e_{l}\subseteq B,~~\forall k\geq 1.

To prove
(\ast)
, we first notice that for
m,j\geq 0
,
e_{j}\cdot e_{m}=\tilde{p}^{j+m}(\tilde{p}-p)^{2}=e^{j+m+1}+\spadesuit
with
\spadesuit
belongs to the
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-subspace in
B
generated by
\{e_{0},\cdots,e_{j+m}\}
. Then we do induction on
k
. The base case
k=1
was explained in the last paragraph. Suppose
(\ast)
holds up to
k
. Then as
f
satisfies the Leibnitz rule
f(xy)=f(x)y+xf(y)+f(x)f(y)
, we have that

\begin{split}f(\tilde{p}^{k+1})=\tilde{p}f(\tilde{p}^{k})+\tilde{p}^{k+1}f(\tilde{p})+f(\tilde{p})f(\tilde{p}^{k})=e_{(k+1)(p+1)+p}+\clubsuit_{k+1}\end{split}

for some
\clubsuit_{k+1}\in\oplus_{l=0}^{(k+1)(p+1)+p-1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}e_{l}\subseteq B
as desired.

Finally by
Eq.

3.11
,

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\WCart,\mathcal{O})=\operatorname{fib}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[\tilde{p}]]\xrightarrow{f}B),

hence
(\ast)
implies that
\mathrm{H}^{0}(\WCart,\mathcal{O})=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
\mathrm{H}^{1}(\WCart,\mathcal{O})\simeq\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N},n\not\equiv p\mod p+1}\mathbb{Z}_{p}.
We finish the proof.
∎

Unfortunately the functor constructed in
Theorem

3.5.2
is not an equivalence. Indeed, note that the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-action on
(A,d)=(\mathbb{Z}_{p}[[q-1]],[p]_{q})
is trivial after modulo
q-1
, hence we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(A)/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{[X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{{\mathrm{dR}}}}
\textstyle{\WCart,}

where
f
is the structure morphism,
\rho_{{\mathrm{dR}}}
is the de Rham point introduced in
[
BL22a
, Example 3.2.6]
. The commutativity of the above diagram follows from the observation that
[p]_{q}
is sent to
p
after modulo
q-1
, hence
\rho
coincides with the de Rham point on the vanishing locus of
q-1
. This diagram implies that the pullback functor along
\pi
actually factors through

F:\mathcal{D}(\WCart)\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])\times_{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})),

which turns out to be an equivalence of categories if we further assume a nilpotence condition on the target.

Theorem 3.5.15
.

Assume
p>2
. Then
F:\mathcal{D}(\WCart)\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])\times_{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))
is fully faithful. Moreover, the essential image of
F
consists of those
\mathcal{E}
in the target whose underlying complex in
\mathcal{D}([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])
(still denoted as
\mathcal{E}
by abuse of notation) satisfies the following additional condition:

•

the
q
-Higgs connection
\partial_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}
of
\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\mathcal{E}|_{X^{\operatorname{HT}}}
can be factored as
e\circ\partial_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}^{\prime}
such that the action of
(\partial_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}^{\prime})^{p}-\partial_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}^{\prime}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}k)
is locally nilpotent, here
e=d^{\prime}(q)\in W(k)[\zeta_{p}]
.

Remark 3.5.16
.

The conditions we impose on the target of
F
are very natural. Actually, we can consider a baby example: the natural projection
\pi:[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\to\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
induces a fully faithful embedding
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))\to\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])
as
[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]
is a tame stack in the sense of
[
AOV08
, Definition 3.1]
, and the essential image of such a pullback functor consists of those
M\in[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]
such that
f^{*}M
has a
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-invariant basis, where
f:\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]
is the covering map. However, such a condition can be checked after reducing to the special fiber
k=\mathbb{F}_{p}
by derived Nakayama lemma, which leads to the following slightly fancy way to state such a result

\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])\times_{\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p})).

Proof of
Theorem

3.5.15
.

We use
\mathcal{C}
to denote the subcategory of
\mathcal{D}([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])\times_{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}))
satisfying the additional nilpotence conditions stated in the theorem. Notice that
F
factors through
\mathcal{C}
by
Proposition

3.5.9
.

Theorem

3.5.2
already shows that
F
is fully faithful, hence it suffices to prove essential surjectivity. As pullback preserves colimit and
\mathcal{D}(\WCart)
is generated under shifts and colimits by the invertible sheaves
\mathcal{I}^{k}
for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
by
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.5.16]
, given the full faithfulness of
F
, it suffices to show the target is also generated by
F(\mathcal{I}^{k})
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}
) under shifts and colimits. For this purpose, it can be reduced to the Hodge-Tate locus. Indeed, if
\mathcal{E}
is in
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{I}^{k},\mathcal{E})\cong\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\mathcal{I}^{-k}\mathcal{E})=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, then we wish to show that
\mathcal{E}=0
, which is equivalent to the vanishing of
\mathcal{E}|_{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}
. Considering the fiber sequence

\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\mathcal{I}^{1-k}\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],\mathcal{I}^{-k}\mathcal{E})\to\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],(\mathcal{I}^{-k}\mathcal{E})|_{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}),

we deduce that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}],(\mathcal{I}^{-k}\mathcal{E})|_{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]})\cong\mathrm{RHom}(\mathcal{I}^{k}|_{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]},\mathcal{E}|_{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]})
vanishes for all
k
. We will show that this already guarantees the vanishing of
\mathcal{E}|_{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}
, denoted as
\mathcal{E}_{0}
later.

For that purpose, it suffices to show that

F_{0}:\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])\times_{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{F}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))

is essential surjective, where
F_{0}
is induced by the restriction of the diagram defining
F
:

\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{F}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{F}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{{\mathrm{dR}}}}
\textstyle{\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}}.}

Indeed, once this is shown, both
\mathcal{I}^{k}
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}
will lie in the essential image of
F_{0}
, hence we could assume that
\mathcal{E}_{0}\cong F_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{0})
for
\mathcal{G}_{0}\in\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})
. The full faithfulness obtained in
Theorem

3.5.2
then implies that

\mathrm{RHom}_{\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}}}(\mathcal{I}^{k},\mathcal{G}_{0})\cong\mathrm{RHom}(\mathcal{I}^{k}|_{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]},\mathcal{E}|_{[X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]})=0

for all
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, hence
\mathcal{G}_{0}\cong 0
as
\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}}
is generated by
\mathcal{I}^{k}
under shifts and colimits by
[
BL22a
, Proposition 3.5.15]
, which finishes the proof.

We are left to prove the essential surjectivity of
F_{0}
. As
\mathcal{D}([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])
is equivalent to the category of
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant quasi-coherent complexes on
X^{\operatorname{HT}}
(for example, see
[
AOV08
, Section 2.1]
), but
\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
is equivalent to the category of

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}(\mathcal{O}_{K}[\partial])
by
Theorem

3.3.4
, then thanks to
Corollary

3.5.8
we see that objects in
\mathcal{D}([X^{\operatorname{HT}}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])
can be described as pairs
(M,\partial_{M})
, where
M
is a
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant
\mathcal{O}_{K}
module equipped with a
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant
q
-Higgs connection
\partial_{M}:M\to M\cdot\epsilon
(
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
acts on
\epsilon
via
Lemma

3.5.6
as usual).

Now given any
\mathcal{H}
in the target of
F_{0}
, it corresponds to the pair
(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{H}),\partial_{\mathcal{H}})
in the above discussion. As
\rho^{*}(\mathcal{H})\in\mathcal{D}([\mathcal{O}_{K}/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])\times_{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{F}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{F}_{p}))
,

\begin{split}\rho^{*}(\mathcal{H})&=N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\quad\text{for}~~N=(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{H}))^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}},\\
(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{H})\cdot\epsilon)^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}&=N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}(\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\epsilon)^{\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}}=N\cdot v\quad\text{for}~~v=e\epsilon.\end{split}

Here the last identity follows from
Lemma

3.5.11
.

As
\partial_{\mathcal{H}}
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivariant, it descends to a
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
-linear morphism
N\xrightarrow{\partial_{N}}N\cdot v
such that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{N}}
\textstyle{N\cdot v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times e}
\textstyle{N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{\mathcal{H}}}
\textstyle{N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{K}\cdot\epsilon.}

By assumption on
\mathcal{H}
,
\partial_{N}
satisfies that
\partial_{N}^{p}-\partial_{N}
is locally nilpotent on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(N\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}\mathbb{F}_{p})
. By unwinding all of the constructions and applying
Proposition

3.5.9
, we see that
(N,\partial_{N})
determines an object
\mathcal{N}\in\mathcal{D}(\WCart^{\operatorname{HT}})
. such that
F_{0}(\mathcal{N})\cong\mathcal{H}
, from which we finish the proof of essential surjectivity of
F_{0}
.
∎

As a quick corollary, we get the following concrete description of
\mathcal{D}(\WCart)
.

Corollary 3.5.17
.

Assume that
p>2
. Then
F
in
Theorem

3.5.15
induces a fully faithful functor

F:\mathcal{D}(\WCart)\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])\times_{[\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}]}\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})).

Here
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
acts on
A[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}]
by extending
\gamma_{u}
on
A
and further requiring that

\gamma_{u}(\partial)=q^{1-[u]}\frac{q-1}{q^{[u]}-1}\partial.

Moreover, the essential image of
F
consists of those
\mathcal{E}
in the target whose underlying complex in
\mathcal{D}([\mathrm{Spf}(A[\partial;\gamma_{A},\partial_{A}])/\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}])
(still denoted as
\mathcal{E}
by abuse of notation) satisfies the following additional condition:

•

the action of
\partial
on
\mathcal{E}_{0}:=\mathcal{E}|_{X^{\operatorname{HT}}}
can be factored as
e\circ\partial^{\prime}
such that the action of
(\partial^{\prime})^{p}-\partial^{\prime}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes^{\mathbb{L}}k)
is locally nilpotent, here
e=d^{\prime}(q)\in W(k)[\zeta_{p}]
.

Proof.

By
Corollary

3.5.8
,
\gamma_{b,c}
is
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-equivaraint. For
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, unwinding our construction of
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
on
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
and our regulation of the
\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
-action on
\epsilon
in
Lemma

3.5.6
, we see that for
u\in\mathbb{F}_{p}^{\times}
,

\gamma_{u}(\partial_{\mathcal{E}})=q^{1-[u]}\frac{q-1}{q^{[u]}-1}\partial_{\mathcal{E}}.

The statement of the corollary then follows by combining
Theorem

3.3.4
with
Theorem

3.5.15
.
∎

4.
Complexes on the relative prismatization over a
q
-prism base

In this section we work in the following setting:
(A,I)
is a transversal prism living over the
q
-prism
(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q})
. Hence
I
is generated by
d=[p]_{q}
(viewed as an element in
A
). We still denote
\frac{q^{p}-1}{d}=q-1\in A
as
\beta
. Let
X
be a
p
-adic smooth formal scheme over
A/I
, we would like to classify quasi-coherent complexes on
X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
and
X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
.

Remark 4.0.1
.

In the last section we work with the (generalized)
q
-prism by allowing
\alpha>0
. In this section since we work with the relative setting, and actually
(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}})
could be viewed as a prism over
(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q})
via

\begin{split}f:(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q})&\longrightarrow(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}})\\
q&\longmapsto q^{p^{\alpha}}\end{split}

hence all of the results in this section works for
(A,I)=(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}})
by replacing
q
with
q^{p^{\alpha}}
in the statements.

4.1.
Local computation

We consider
X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)
, a smooth
p
-adic formal scheme over
\overline{A}=A/I
. We assume that
X
is a small affine, in the sense that we fix a
p
-completely étale chart

\square:=\overline{A}\left\langle T_{1},\ldots,T_{m}\right\rangle\rightarrow R

By deformation theory, this étale chart map uniquely lifts to a prism
(\tilde{R},I)
over
(A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle,I)
, here
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
is equipped with the
\delta
structure respecting that on
A
and sending
T_{i}
to
0
, then we apply
[
BS21
, Lemma 2.18]
to uniquely extend such a
\delta
-structure to
\tilde{R}
. Moreover, the induced map
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\to\tilde{R}
is
(p,I)
-completely étale. In particular,
(\tilde{R},I)
is a prism in
(X/A)_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
and
\tilde{R}/I=R
. In this case,
\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}=\tilde{R}\otimes_{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle}\Omega_{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle/A}
is a finite free
R
-module of rank
m
with a basis given by
\{dT_{1},\cdots,dT_{m}\}
.

For any
i\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
, we define the automorphism
\gamma_{i}
of
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
fixing
A
and sending
T_{i}
to
q^{p}T_{i}
and
T_{j}
to
T_{j}
for
j\neq i
. As
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\to\tilde{R}
is
(p,I)
-completely étale, these automorphisms lift uniquely to automorphisms
\gamma_{i}
of
\tilde{R}
. Also,
\gamma_{i}
on
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
is congruent to
1
modulo the topologically nilpotent ideal
(q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i})
, hence so is
\gamma_{i}
on
\tilde{R}
, which enables us to
define a twisted version of
q
-connection studied in
[
Sch17
]
. Namely, for
f\in\tilde{R}
, we set

\nabla_{i}(f)=d\frac{\gamma_{i}(f)-f}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}},\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla(f)=d\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(f)-f}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i}\in\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}.

Lemma 4.1.1
.

Let
S=\tilde{R}\oplus\epsilon\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}
and we promote
S
to an
A
-algebra by requiring that

(\epsilon dT_{i})^{2}=\beta T_{i}\cdot\epsilon dT_{i},\quad\epsilon dT_{i}\cdot\epsilon dT_{j}=0~~\text{for}~~i\neq j.

Then the map

\begin{split}\psi:\tilde{R}&\longrightarrow S\\
f&\longmapsto f+\epsilon\nabla_{q}(f)=f+\epsilon\cdot d\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(f)-f}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i}\end{split}

defines a ring homomorphism of
A
-algebras, where the canonical
A
algebra structure on
S
is induced by
A\to S
sending
a
to
a
.

Proof.

Clearly
\psi
is
A
-linear (as
\gamma_{i}
’s are all
A
-linear) and additive, we just need to check that
\psi_{x}(f_{1}f_{2})=\psi(f_{1})\cdot\psi(f_{2})
for
f_{1},f_{2}\in\tilde{R}
. But we have that

\begin{split}\psi(f_{1})\cdot\psi(f_{2})&=(f_{1}+\epsilon\cdot d\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(f_{1})-f_{1}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i})\cdot(f_{2}+\epsilon\cdot d\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(f_{2})-f_{2}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i})\\
&=f_{1}f_{2}+d\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{dT_{i}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}(f_{1}(\gamma_{i}(f_{2})-f_{2})+f_{2}(\gamma_{i}(f_{1})-f_{1})+(\gamma_{i}(f_{1})-f_{1})(\gamma_{i}(f_{2})-f_{2}))\\
&=f_{1}f_{2}+d\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{dT_{i}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}(\gamma_{i}(f_{1}f_{2})-f_{1}f_{2})=\psi(f_{1}f_{2}).\end{split}

Here the second identity holds as
\epsilon dT_{i}\cdot\epsilon dT_{j}=0
for
i\neq j
and

\begin{split}&(d\epsilon\frac{\gamma_{i}(f_{1})-f_{1}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i})\cdot(d\epsilon\frac{\gamma_{i}(f_{2})-f_{2}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i})=d^{2}(\beta T_{i}\cdot\epsilon dT_{i})\frac{(\gamma_{i}(f_{1})-f_{1})(\gamma_{i}(f_{2})-f_{2})}{(q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i})^{2}}\\
=&\frac{d\epsilon dT_{i}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}(T_{i}\beta d\frac{(\gamma_{i}(f_{1})-f_{1})(\gamma_{i}(f_{2})-f_{2})}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}})=\frac{d\epsilon dT_{i}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}\cdot(\gamma_{i}(f_{1})-f_{1})(\gamma_{i}(f_{2})-f_{2}),\end{split}

where the last equality follows from
\beta d=q^{p}-1
by definition.
∎

Remark 4.1.2
.

For the reasons that will be clear later, we would like to point out that the above statements still hold if we replace
\psi
by
\psi_{i}
defined as follows

\begin{split}\psi_{i}:\tilde{R}&\longrightarrow S_{i}=\tilde{R}[\epsilon_{i}]/(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\beta T_{i}\epsilon_{i})=\tilde{R}\oplus\tilde{R}\cdot\epsilon_{i}\\
f&\longmapsto f+\epsilon_{i}\nabla_{i}(f)=f+\epsilon_{i}\cdot d\frac{\gamma_{i}(f)-f}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}\end{split}

Moreover, one can verify that
\nabla_{i}\circ\nabla_{j}=\nabla_{j}\circ\nabla_{i}
for
i\neq j
, which essentially follows from the commutativity of
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
, i.e.
\gamma_{i}\circ\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{j}\circ\gamma_{i}
.

Remark 4.1.3
.

We can upgrade
S
to a
\delta
-ring extending that structure on
\tilde{R}
by requiring that
\varphi(\epsilon dT_{i})=T_{i}^{p-1}\frac{q^{p}-1}{q-1}\epsilon dT_{i}
. Actually, one can calculate that

\varphi(\epsilon dT_{i})-(\epsilon dT_{i})^{p}=T_{i}^{p-1}\epsilon dT_{i}(\frac{q^{p}-1}{q-1}-(q-1)^{p-1})

is zero modulo
p
, hence
\varphi
is indeed a lift of the Fribenius modulo
p
. Moreover, under such a regulation
\psi
defined in
Lemma

4.1.1
is actually a
\delta
-ring homomorphism, see next proposition.

Proposition 4.1.4
.

\psi
(resp.
\psi_{i}
) constructed in
Lemma

4.1.1
(resp.
Remark

4.1.2
) is actually a
\delta
-ring homomorphism, where the target is equipped with the
\delta
-structure given in
Remark

4.1.3
.

Proof.

We only prove the statement for
\psi
. First we claim that it suffices to show that
\psi:A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\to S
preserves the
\delta
-structure. Indeed, once this is shown, the two
\delta
-structures on
\psi(\tilde{R})
, among which one is induced by that on
\tilde{R}
and the other is inherited from
S
, must coincide by
[
BS22
, Lemma 2.18]
as both of them extend
\delta
-structure on
\psi(A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle)
(here we notice that
\tilde{R}\hat{\otimes}_{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle,\psi}A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\xrightarrow[\psi]{\simeq}\psi(\tilde{R})
is
(p,I)
-completely étale over
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
). Given that claim, we are left to show that
\psi(\varphi(T_{i}))=\varphi(\psi(T_{i}))
for all
1\leq i\leq m
as both sides of
\psi
are
p
-torsion free. But as
d=\frac{q^{p}-1}{q-1}
and
\varphi(q)=q^{p}
, this follows from

\begin{split}\psi(\varphi(T_{i}))&=\psi(T_{i}^{p})=T_{i}^{p}+d\epsilon\frac{(q^{p}T_{i})^{p}-T_{i}^{p}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i}=T_{i}^{p}+\frac{q^{p^{2}}-1}{q-1}T_{i}^{p-1}\epsilon dT_{i},\\
\varphi(\psi(T_{i}))&=\varphi(T_{i}+d\epsilon dT_{i})=T_{i}^{p}+\epsilon\frac{q^{p^{2}}-1}{q^{p}-1}T_{i}^{p-1}\frac{q^{p}-1}{q-1}\epsilon dT_{i}=T_{i}^{p}+\frac{q^{p^{2}}-1}{q-1}T_{i}^{p-1}\epsilon dT_{i}.\end{split}

∎

Next proposition specifies a homotopy between
\tilde{\psi}:\tilde{R}\to W(S)/d
and
\tilde{\iota}:\tilde{R}\to W(S)/d
, which will be used later to construct the desired
q
-Higgs derivation.

Proposition 4.1.5
.

Let
S=\tilde{R}\oplus\epsilon\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}
as in
Lemma

4.1.1
, then for any
x\in\tilde{R}
there exists a unique
c_{\psi}(x)
in
W(S)
such that

\tilde{\psi}(x)-\tilde{\iota}(x)=d\cdot c_{\psi}(x).

Here
\tilde{\psi}
is the unique
\delta
-ring morphism such that the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

and
\tilde{\iota}
is defined similarly with the bottom line in the above diagram replaced with the natural inclusion
\iota:\tilde{R}\hookrightarrow S
.

Proof.

As
W(S)
is
d
-torsion free by
Lemma

4.1.6
, it suffices to show existence. First we would like to reduce to the case that
x\in A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\to\tilde{R}
. For this purpose, we consider the following two commutative diagrams:

\textstyle{\tilde{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\iota}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S/d}
\textstyle{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\iota}}
\textstyle{W(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{W(S)/d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and

\textstyle{\tilde{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\psi}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S/d}
\textstyle{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\psi}}
\textstyle{W(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{W(S)/d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Once the statement is proven for
x\in A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
, then both
\pi\circ\tilde{\iota}
and
\pi\circ\tilde{\psi}
make the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\tilde{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi=\iota}
\textstyle{S/d}
\textstyle{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\psi}=\tilde{\iota}}
\textstyle{W(S)/d\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

However, as
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\to\tilde{R}
is formally étale and
W(S)/d\to S/d
is a pro-thickening, such lift must be unique, hence
\pi\circ\tilde{\iota}=\pi\circ\tilde{\psi}
, the desired result follows.

To verify the existence of
c_{\psi}(x)
for
x\in A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
, without loss of generality, we can assume
x=T_{i}
as the set of
x
satisfying the statements is closed under addition and multiplication. For simplicity, we omit
i
from the subscript. We wish to construct
c=(c_{0},c_{1},\ldots)
such that
\tilde{\psi}(T)-\tilde{\iota}(T)=\tilde{\iota}(d)\cdot c
. As
S
is
p
-torsion free, the ghost map is injective, hence this identity is equivalent to that

(4.1)

\displaystyle\forall n\geq 0,w_{n}(\tilde{\psi}(T))-w_{n}(\tilde{\iota}(T))=w_{n}(c)\cdot w_{n}(\tilde{\iota}(d)),

where
w_{n}
denotes the
n
-th ghost map.
Notice that

\begin{split}&w_{n}(\tilde{\iota}(T))=w_{0}(\varphi^{n}(\tilde{\iota}(T)))=w_{0}(\tilde{\iota}(\varphi^{n}(T)))=T^{p^{n}},\\
&w_{n}(\tilde{\psi}(T))=w_{0}(\varphi^{n}(\tilde{\psi}(T)))=w_{0}(\tilde{\psi}(\varphi^{n}(T)))=\psi(T^{p^{n}})=T^{p^{n}}+\epsilon\cdot d\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(T^{p^{n}})-T^{p^{n}}}{q^{p}T-T}dT,\\
&w_{n}(\tilde{\iota}(d))=\varphi^{n}(d)=[p]_{q^{p^{n}}}.\end{split}

Consequently,

\begin{split}&w_{n}(\tilde{\psi}(T))-w_{n}(\tilde{\iota}(T))=\epsilon\cdot d\frac{q^{p^{n+1}}T^{p^{n}}-T^{p^{n}}}{q^{p}T-T}dT\\
=&\epsilon d\cdot T^{p^{n}-1}\frac{q^{p^{n+1}}-1}{q^{p}-1}dT\\
=&\epsilon T^{p^{n}-1}\frac{q^{p}-1}{q-1}\cdot\frac{q^{p^{n+1}}-1}{q^{p^{n}}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{p^{n}}-1}{q^{p}-1}dT\\
=&\epsilon T^{p^{n}-1}[p]_{q^{p^{n}}}\cdot[p^{n}]_{q}dT\end{split}

We define
r_{n}=T^{p^{n}-1}[p^{n}]_{q}\cdot\epsilon dT
. As
S
is
\varphi^{n}(d)
-torsion free for all
n
,
Eq.

4.1
holds if and only if the ghost coordinates of
c
is precisely given by
(r_{0},r_{1},\cdots)
. In other words, we aim to show that
(r_{0},r_{1},\cdots)\in S^{\mathbb{N}}
is the ghost coordinate for some element
c\in W(S)
(such
c
is unique if exists as ghost map is injective). As
S
is a
\delta
-ring thanks to
Remark

4.1.3
, by invoking Dwork’s lemma (see
[
Laz06
, 4.6 on page 213]
for details), it suffices to show that for any
n\geq 0
,

p^{n+1}|\varphi(r_{n})-r_{n+1}.

But
\varphi(r_{n})-r_{n+1}
actually vanishes as

\begin{split}&\varphi(r_{n})-r_{n+1}=\varphi(T^{p^{n}-1}[p^{n}]_{q}\cdot\epsilon dT)-T^{p^{n+1}-1}[p^{n+1}]_{q}\cdot\epsilon dT\\
=&T^{p-1}\frac{q^{p}-1}{q-1}T^{p^{n+1}-p}\cdot\frac{q^{p^{n+1}}-1}{q^{p}-1}\cdot\epsilon dT-T^{p^{n+1}-1}\cdot\frac{q^{p^{n+1}}-1}{q-1}\cdot\epsilon dT\\
=&0.\end{split}

Hence we finish the proof.
∎

The following lemma is used in the above proof.

Lemma 4.1.6
.

Let
S
be as above, then
W(S)
is
d
-torsion free.

Proof.

As
S
is
p
-torsion free, the ghost map is injective, hence it suffices to show
\varphi^{n}(d)
is a non-zero divisor in
S
, which follows from
[
ALB20
, Lemma 3.3]
.
∎

Now we are ready to construct the
q
-Higgs derivation for quasi-coherent complexes on
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
, based on the following key proposition.

Proposition 4.1.7
.

c_{\psi}
constructed in
Proposition

4.1.5
induces an isomorphism
\gamma_{c_{\psi}}
between functors
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(S)\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\rightarrow X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}

and
\rho\circ\psi:\mathrm{Spf}(S)\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\rightarrow X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
, i.e. we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{c_{\psi}}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}}

Proof.

By abuse of notation we write
\iota:A\to S
for the composition of
A\to\tilde{R}\xrightarrow{\iota}S
, then
\rho(S)
corresponds to the point

(\alpha:(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W(S)\to W(S),\eta:\Cone((d)\to\tilde{R})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\iota}}\Cone(\alpha))

in
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(S)
, while
(\rho\circ\psi)(S)
corresponds to the point

(\alpha:(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W(S)\to W(S),\eta^{\prime}:\Cone((d)\to\tilde{R})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\psi}}\Cone(\alpha)).

Then clearly
c_{\psi}
constructed in
Proposition

4.1.5
serves a homotopy between
\eta^{\prime}
and
\eta
by drawing the following diagram as maps of quasi-ideals (in the sense of
[
Dri20
]
), which finishes the proof.

\textstyle{(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\tilde{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{W(S).}

∎

Let
?\in\{\emptyset,1,\cdots,m\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Recall that we have
\psi_{?}:\tilde{R}\to S_{?}
constructed in
Lemma

4.1.1
and
Remark

4.1.2
. Then we are ready to construct a
q
-Higgs derivation
\nabla_{\mathcal{E},?}
on
\rho^{*}\mathscr{E}
for
\mathscr{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
(resp.
\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
). Based on
Proposition

4.1.7
, we have an isomorphism
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{?}}}:\rho\circ\psi_{?}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho
. Consequently, for
\mathscr{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
(resp.
\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
), we have an isomorphism

\gamma_{c_{\psi_{?}}}:\psi_{?}^{*}\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathscr{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S_{?}.

Unwinding the definitions, this could be identified with a
\psi_{?}
-linear morphism

(4.2)

\displaystyle\gamma_{c_{\psi_{?}}}:\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E})\rightarrow\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E})\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S_{?}.

Moreover, our definition of
c_{\psi_{?}}
in
Proposition

4.1.5
implies that
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{?}}}
in
Eq.

4.2
reduces to the identity modulo
\epsilon_{?}
, hence could be written as
\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{?}\nabla_{\mathscr{E},?}
for some operator
\nabla_{\mathscr{E},?}:\rho^{*}\mathscr{E}\rightarrow\rho^{*}\mathscr{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A,?}
(as
S_{?}=\tilde{R}\oplus\epsilon\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A,?}
). Moreover, the construction implies that
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}\otimes dT_{i}
. We will call
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}
the
q
-Higgs derivation
on the complex
\rho^{*}\mathscr{E}
.

Next we show that for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
,
(\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E}),\gamma_{c_{\psi}}=\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})
actually enjoys an additional property.

Proposition 4.1.8
.

For
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
,
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}
on
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
satisfies the following property:

\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}:\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}^{2}

vanishes.

Proof.

As
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}\otimes dT_{i}
, it suffices to show that
\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}\circ\nabla_{\mathcal{E},j}=\nabla_{\mathcal{E},j}\circ\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}
for
i\neq j
. We may assume that
i=1
and
j=2
. For
i=1,2
, let
S_{i}=\tilde{R}[\epsilon_{i}]/(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\beta T_{i}\epsilon_{i})=\tilde{R}\oplus\tilde{R}\cdot\epsilon_{i}
and
\psi_{i}:\tilde{R}\to S_{i}=\tilde{R}[\epsilon_{i}]
be as in
Remark

4.1.2
. Denote
T=S_{1}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S_{2}
and let
\psi_{2}^{\prime}:S_{1}\to T
be the base change of
\psi_{2}
, i.e.
\psi_{2}^{\prime}(x_{1}+\epsilon_{1}y_{1})=(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{2}\nabla_{q,2})\circ(x_{1}+\epsilon_{1}y_{1})=x_{1}+\epsilon_{1}y_{1}+\epsilon_{2}(\nabla_{q,2}(x_{1}))+\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\nabla_{q,2}(y_{1})
. Similarly we define
\psi_{1}^{\prime}:S_{2}\to T
be the base change of
\psi_{1}
.

We consider the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{2}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(S_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(S_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{c_{\psi_{1}}}}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{c_{\psi_{2}}}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}.}

Notice that

\rho\circ\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}^{\prime}\xrightarrow[\gamma_{c_{\psi_{1}}}]{\simeq}\rho\circ\psi_{2}^{\prime}=\rho\circ\psi_{2}\xrightarrow[\gamma_{c_{\psi_{2}}}]{\simeq}\rho,

here the last
\rho
actually means
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(T)\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\to X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
, and the first map is the canonical morphism. Hence it induces
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{1,2}}}:\psi_{2}^{\prime,*}(\psi_{1}^{*}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}))\cong\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}T
, which by unwinding definitions is the
T
-linear extension of

\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}\otimes 1}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R},\psi_{1}}S_{1}\xrightarrow[\gamma_{c_{\psi_{1}}}]{\simeq}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S_{1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}\otimes 1}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S_{1})\otimes_{S_{1},\psi_{2}^{\prime}}T=\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R},\psi_{2}}T\xrightarrow[\gamma_{c_{\psi_{2}}}]{\simeq}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}T.

As
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{1}}}=\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{1}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1}
and
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{1}}}=\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{1}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2}
, the above morphism
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}T
is given by

(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{2}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2})\circ(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{1}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1})=(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{1}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1}+\epsilon_{2}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2}+\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2}\circ\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1}).

On the other hand, as the left-upper square is commutative by
Remark

4.1.2
,
\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}^{\prime}=\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}^{\prime}
, repeating the argument via
\rho\circ\psi_{2}\circ\psi_{1}^{\prime}
instead, we see that
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{1,2}}}
should also be given the
T
-linear base change of

(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{1}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1})\circ(\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{2}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2})=\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon_{1}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1}+\epsilon_{2}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2}+\epsilon_{1}\epsilon_{2}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1}\circ\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2},

which forces
\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2}\circ\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1}=\nabla_{\mathcal{E},1}\circ\nabla_{\mathcal{E},2}
(as
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{1,2}}}
is the unique homotopy determined by the two
\tilde{R}
-algebra structures on
T
, given by
\iota
and
\psi_{2}^{\prime}\circ\psi_{1}=\psi_{1}^{\prime}\circ\psi_{2}
separately).
∎

In the special case that
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})^{\heartsuit}
,
\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E})
is a
\tilde{R}
-module concentrated on degree
0
and we observe that
\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}
satisfies certain twisted Leibnitz rule.

Lemma 4.1.9

(Twisted Leibnitz rule on the
q
-Higgs derivation)
.

Given a pair
(M,\gamma_{M})
such that
M=\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E})
as above and
\gamma_{M}=\gamma_{c_{\psi}}:M\otimes_{\tilde{R},\psi}S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\rightarrow}}M\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S
, write
\gamma_{M}
as
\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon\nabla_{M}
when restricted to
M
(hence
\nabla_{M}:M\to M\otimes_{\tilde{R}}\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}
), then
\nabla_{M}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla_{M,i}\otimes dT_{i}
, and
\nabla_{M,i}
satisfies the following twisted Leibnitz rule:

\nabla_{M,i}(ax)=a\nabla_{M,i}(x)+\nabla_{i}(a)x+\beta T_{i}\nabla_{i}(a)\nabla_{M,i}(x)=\gamma_{i}(a)\nabla_{M,i}(x)+\nabla_{i}(a)x

for
a\in\tilde{R}
and
x\in M
, here
\nabla_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}
on
\tilde{R}
is defined above
Lemma

4.1.1
. In particular,

\nabla_{M,i}(T_{i}x)=T_{i}\nabla_{M,i}(x)+dx+\beta T_{i}d\nabla_{M,i}(x)=dx+T_{i}q^{p}\nabla_{M,i}(x).

Proof.

For such a pair
(M,\gamma_{M})
,

\begin{split}&\gamma_{M}(ax)=\psi(a)\gamma_{M}(x)=(a+\epsilon\nabla(a))(x+\epsilon\nabla_{M}(x))\\
=&(a+\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla_{i}(a)dT_{i})(x+\epsilon\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla_{i}(x)dT_{i})\\
=&ax+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon dT_{i}(\nabla_{i}(a)x+a\nabla_{i}(x))+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon\beta T_{i}\nabla_{i}(a)\nabla_{i}(x)\cdot dT_{i}\\
=&ax+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\epsilon dT_{i}(\nabla_{i}(a)x+a\nabla_{i}(x)+\beta T_{i}\nabla_{i}(a)\nabla_{i}(x)),\end{split}

here the second equation follows from
Lemma

4.1.1
. This implies the desired result.
∎

Remark 4.1.10
.

More generally, let
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
be quasi-coherent complexes on
X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
, and let
\mathscr{E}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\prime}
denote their derived tensor product. Then the same argument shows that the
q
-Higgs derivation
\nabla_{\mathscr{E}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\prime},i}
on
\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E}\otimes\mathscr{E}^{\prime})\cong\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E})\otimes\rho^{*}(\mathscr{E}^{\prime})
can be identified with
\nabla_{\mathscr{E},i}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathscr{E}^{\prime}}+\mathrm{Id}_{\mathscr{E}}\otimes\nabla_{\mathscr{E}^{\prime},i}+\beta T_{i}\nabla_{\mathscr{E},i}\otimes\nabla_{\mathscr{E}^{\prime},i}
.

Motivated by
Proposition

4.1.8
and
Lemma

4.1.9
, it is natural to introduce the following non-commutative ring.

Definition 4.1.11
.

Let
\gamma_{\tilde{R},i}:\tilde{R}\to\tilde{R}
be the ring automorphism
\gamma_{i}
defined above
Lemma

4.1.1
, then
\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}:\tilde{R}\to\tilde{R}
, denoted as
\nabla_{i}
above
Lemma

4.1.1
, is a
\gamma_{\tilde{R},i}
-derivation of
\tilde{R}
, i.e.
\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}(x_{1}x_{2})=\gamma_{\tilde{R},i}(x_{1})\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}(x_{2})+\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}(x_{1})x_{2}
. We define the
Ore extension

\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
to be the noncommutative ring obtained by giving the ring of polynomials
\tilde{R}[\nabla_{1},\cdots,\nabla_{m}]
a new multiplication law, subject to the identity

\begin{split}\nabla_{i}\cdot\nabla_{j}&=\nabla_{j}\cdot\nabla_{i},~~~\forall~1\leq i,j\leq m\\
\nabla_{i}\cdot r&=\gamma_{\tilde{R},i}(r)\cdot\nabla_{i}+\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}(r),~~~\forall~r\in\tilde{R},1\leq i\leq m.\end{split}

Remark 4.1.12
.

Note that
\tilde{R}
is isomorphic to the quotient of
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
by the (left) ideal generated by
\nabla_{i}~(1\leq i\leq m)
. In this sense,
\tilde{R}
is a left
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
-module and
\nabla_{i}
acts on
R
via
\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}
as desired. Moreover, the Koszul complex provides a canonical resolution for
\tilde{R}
by finite free
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
-modules:

[\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\nabla_{1},\cdots,\nabla_{m})}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq m}\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]\cdots\to\bigoplus_{1\leq k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}\leq m}\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]\to\cdots]\simeq\tilde{R}[0],

where
\bigoplus_{1\leq k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}\leq m}\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
lives in (cohomological) degree
s-m
and the differential from
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
in spot
k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}
to
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
in spot
p_{1}<\cdots<p_{s+1}
is nonzero only if
\{k_{1},\cdots,k_{s}\}\subseteq\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s+1}\}
, in which case it sends
f\in\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
to
(-1)^{u-1}f\cdot\nabla_{p_{u}}
, where
u\in\{1,\cdots,s+1\}
is the unique integer such that
p_{u}\notin\{k_{1},\cdots,k_{s}\}
.

Consequently, for
N
an object in
\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}])
, the derived category of (left)
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
-modules, we have that

\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}])}(\tilde{R},N)\simeq[N\xrightarrow{\nabla_{N}:=\sum\nabla_{i}\otimes dT_{i}}N\otimes_{\tilde{R}}\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{N}\wedge\nabla_{N}}\cdots\to N\otimes_{\tilde{R}}\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}^{m}\to 0].

We will call the right-hand side of the above equation the
q
-de Rham complex of
N
, denoted as
\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla)
. Such an observation will be used to calculate the cohomology later.

Given
Definition

4.1.11
, the discussion in this subsection so far can be then summarized as follows:

Proposition 4.1.13
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the pullback along
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R}/d^{n})\to X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
induces a functor

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})&\to\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}])\\
\mathcal{E}&\mapsto(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})\end{split}

which will be denoted as
\beta_{n}^{+}
later.

Proof.

Given
Proposition

4.1.8
and
Lemma

4.1.9
, the proof of
Proposition

3.1.21
still works here, provided we know that
\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
is equivalent to the derived category of its heart. But the latter can be proven similarly as
Proposition

3.1.20
by reducing to the Hodge-Tate locus and then taking
[
AHLB23
, Theorem 6.3]
as an input.
∎

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. As for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the global section of
\mathcal{E}
is defined as

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E}):=\lim_{f:\mathrm{Spec}(R)\to X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}}f^{*}\mathcal{E}

In particular, the cover
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R}/d^{n})\rightarrow X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
induces a natural morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}.

The next proposition shows that it actually factors through the fiber of
\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}
for all
i
.

Proposition 4.1.14
.

For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the natural morphism
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
induces a canonical morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}).

for all
i
. Hence it induces a canonical morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}/I^{n}}\Omega_{(\tilde{R}/I^{n})/(A/I^{n})}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}/I^{n}}\Omega_{(\tilde{R}/I^{n})/(A/I^{n})}^{m}\to 0)

Proof.

By the discussion after
Proposition

4.1.7
, we have an isomorphism
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{i}}}:\rho\circ\psi_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho
as functors
\mathrm{Spf}(S_{i}/I^{n})\rightarrow X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
. Then the definition of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})
implies that the natural morphism
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}
factors through the equalizer of

\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{Id}\otimes 1}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}/d^{n}}S/d^{n}

and

\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\gamma_{c_{\psi_{i}}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}/d^{n},\psi}S/d^{n},

where
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{i}}}=\mathrm{Id}+\epsilon\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}
. This produces a canonical morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\to\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}).

for arbitrary
i
. Then the last statement follows as the de Rham complex of
\mathcal{E}
is the totalization of certain finite diagram admitting a morphism from
\prod_{i=1}^{m}\operatorname{fib}(\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i})
.
∎

Our goal is to prove that
\beta_{n}^{+}
stated in
Proposition

4.1.13
identifies the source with a certain subcategory of the target. For that purpose, similar to the absolute case, we would like to understand the behavior of
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}
when restricted to the Hodge-Tate locus first. If we work with canonical Higgs field instead of
q
-Higgs derivation, this was discussed in
[
AHLB23
, Section 6.1]
using Fourier transform. However, for our purpose, it is more convenient to follow the strategy of
[
BL22a
, Theorem 3.5.8]
, in the spirit of
[
AHLB23
, Remark 6.14]
.

Actually, as
\psi:\tilde{R}\to S
reduces to the natural embedding after modulo
d
, we see
\gamma_{c_{\psi}}
descends to an automorphism of

X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}\times_{\mathrm{Spf}(R)}\mathrm{Spf}(R[\epsilon\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}]/((\epsilon dT_{i})^{2}-\beta T_{i}\epsilon dT_{i})),

which implies that for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}})
,
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}
also descends to a functor from
\mathcal{E}
to itself.

To simplify the notation, we write
\epsilon_{i}
for
\epsilon dT_{i}
in the following and hence

S/d=R[\epsilon_{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{m}]/(\epsilon_{i}^{2}-\beta T_{i}\epsilon_{i},\epsilon_{i}\epsilon_{j})_{1\leq i<j\leq m}\cong R\oplus\oplus_{i=1}^{m}R\epsilon_{i}.

As
X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}
is the classifying stack of
T_{X/\bar{A}}^{\sharp}\{1\}
over
X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)
by
[
BL22b
, Proposition 5.12]
, and we have already fixed a generalizer
d
of
I
as well as a basis for
\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}
, this group can be trivialized as
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}
.

Under such an identification,
\gamma_{c_{\psi}}
corresponds to an element in
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}(S/d)
, and we claim this element is precisely
(\epsilon_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}\in(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}(S/d)
.
19
19

19

Here by our definition we see that
\epsilon_{i}^{k}=T_{i}^{k-1}\beta^{k-1}\epsilon_{i}
. In particular, it doesn’t vanish for
k\geq 1
. However, the divided powers of
\epsilon_{i}
still exist in
S/d
as
v_{p}(\beta)=v_{p}(q-1)=\frac{1}{p-1}
, hence
v_{p}(\beta^{n-1})=\frac{n-1}{p-1}\geq v_{p}(n!)
. Consequently,
\epsilon_{i}\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}(S/d)
.
.
To see this, unwinding the construction of
\gamma_{c_{\psi}}
, we just need to verify that the image of
c_{\psi}(T_{i})
under the natural morphism

W(S)\to W(S/d)

lies in
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}(S/d)
and is precisely given by
\epsilon_{i}
. As
S/d
is
p
-torsion free, it suffices to check that the first coordinate for
c_{\psi}(T_{i})
is
\epsilon_{i}
in
S/d
, which is clear from the proof of
Proposition

4.1.5
.

The next lemma is an analog of
Lemma

3.2.2
and helps us understand how
\nabla_{i}
acts on
\rho^{*}\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
under the covering morphism
\rho:X\to X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}
, in parallel to the behavior of the Sen operator
\theta
studied in
[
BL22b
]
and
[
AHLB23
]
(see
[
AHLB23
, Section 6.1]
for details).

Lemma 4.1.15
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
, then

•

\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong R\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\}^{\wedge}_{p}
.

•

the sequence
0\to(R\{a_{j}\}_{j\neq i})^{\wedge}_{p}\to\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{i}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\to 0
is exact.

Proof.

The projection formula tells us that
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong R\{a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\}^{\wedge}_{p}
. To prove the second statement, we write
B
for
(R\{a_{j}\}_{j\neq i})^{\wedge}_{p}
and omit
i
from the subscript of
a_{i}
and
T_{i}
for the ease of notation.
As the formal group law on the
i
-th component of
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}
is given by

\Delta:\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}\hat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}},~~~~a\mapsto a+b

and the isomorphism
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{i}}}
(hence also the
q
-derivation
\nabla_{i}
) is constructed via
\epsilon\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}(S/d)
, hence for
f(a)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{i}\frac{a^{n}}{n!}\in\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}=B\{a\}_{p}^{\wedge}
with
c_{i}\in B
,
\gamma_{c_{\psi_{i}}}(f)=f(a+\epsilon)=f+\epsilon\nabla_{i}(f)
. We then calculate that

\begin{split}\epsilon\nabla_{i}(f)&=f(a+\epsilon)-f(a)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\frac{(a+\epsilon)^{n}-a^{n}}{n!}\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{i}a^{i}\epsilon^{n-i})=\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{i}a^{i}(\beta T)^{n-i-1}),\end{split}

where the last equality follows from
\epsilon^{j}=(\beta T)^{j-1}\epsilon
. Consequently

\begin{split}\nabla_{i}(f)&=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a^{i}(\sum_{n=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}\binom{n}{i}(\beta T)^{n-1-i})\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{i}}{i!}(\sum_{n=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{(n-i)!}(\beta T)^{n-1-i}).\end{split}

We then consider the infinite dimensional matrix
M
with
M_{i,j}=\frac{(\beta T)^{j-i}}{(j+1-i)!}
for
i\leq j
and
0
otherwise, then
M
is an upper triangular matrix with all the diagonal elements being
1
in
B
, hence
M
is an invertible matrix. Let
g=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}\frac{a^{n}}{n!}
, then the previous calculation tells us that
\nabla_{i}(f)=g
if and only if

(4.3)

M\vec{c}=\vec{b}.

for
\vec{b}=(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots)^{\mathrm{T}}
and
\vec{c}=(c_{1},c_{2},\cdots)^{\mathrm{T}}
.
As
M
is invertible, given any
\vec{b}
satisfies that
b_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
, there exists a unique
\vec{c}
solving
Eq.

4.3
. Moreover,
c_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
. This finishes the proof of the second result stated in the proposition.
∎

As a quick corollary of
Lemma

4.1.15
, we get the following Poincare lemma for
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Corollary 4.1.16
.

The de Rham complex for
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
formed via
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}
is a resolution for
\mathcal{O}_{X}
. More precisely,

R\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}^{m}\to 0).

Proof.

By
Lemma

4.1.15
,
(R\{a_{j}\}_{j\neq i})^{\wedge}_{p}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{fib}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0},i}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0})
. Moreover, the proof of it actually implies that for any
1<q\leq m
,
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0},q}
is surjective on
\cap_{j=1}^{q-1}\ker(\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0},j})
. By a well-known lemma, the Koszul complex for
\{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0},i}\}_{1\leq i\leq m}
is hence concentrated on degree
0
and is given by
\cap_{j=1}^{m}\ker(\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0},j})
. Consequently by taking limits with respect to all
i
, we get that

R\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}^{m}\to 0).

∎

Proposition 4.1.17
.

Assume
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\rho_{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
as in the previous corollary. For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}})
, there is a canonical resolution

\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\rho_{*}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho_{*}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{}\rho_{*}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}^{m}\to 0).

Moreover, taking cohomology induces a canonical quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}^{m}\to 0).

Hence the morphism constructed in
Proposition

4.1.14
is a quasi-isomorphism when
n=1
.

Proof.

By the discussion above
Lemma

4.1.15
,
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}
descends to a functor from
\mathcal{E}_{0}
to
\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}
and hence we have a morphism

\mathcal{O}_{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}}\to(\mathcal{E}_{0}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}^{m}\to 0),

which actually is a resolution for
\mathcal{O}_{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}}
by
Corollary

4.1.16
and faithfully flat descent. For a general
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
, tensoring
\mathcal{E}
with the above sequence and then yields a canonical resolution for
\mathcal{E}

\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\mathcal{E}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}\otimes\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}^{m}\to 0)

By the usual trick of trivializing Hopf algebra’s comodules, the projection formula
\rho_{*}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\rho_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
identifies
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}
with
\mathrm{Id}\otimes\nabla_{\mathcal{E},0}
, hence applying it and then taking cohomology we then get a canonical quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\Omega_{R/\bar{A}}^{m}\to 0).

∎

Remark 4.1.18
.

The analog of
Proposition

4.1.17
by replacing the
q
-Higgs derivation with the usual canonical Higgs filed (see
[
AHLB23
, Definition 6.15]
) is
[
AHLB23
, Lemma 6.10]
.

Example 4.1.19

(
q
-Higgs derivations on the structure sheaf when restricted to the Hodge-Tate locus)
.

For
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}}
,
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}=0
. Indeed, as in
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.5.14]
,
Proposition

4.1.17
implies that
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}}
if and only if
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
and
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}=0
.

Now we proceed to classify quasi-coherent sheaves on
X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
for all
n
.

Proposition 4.1.20
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the natural morphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}/I^{n}}\Omega_{(\tilde{R}/I^{n})/(A/I^{n})}\to\cdots\xrightarrow{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}}\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\tilde{R}/I^{n}}\Omega_{(\tilde{R}/I^{n})/(A/I^{n})}^{m}\to 0)

constructed in
Proposition

4.1.14
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, it reduces to
n=1
by standard dévissage, which follows from
Proposition

4.1.17
.

Finally for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, as taking global sections commutes with limits, by writing
\mathcal{E}
as the inverse limit of
\mathcal{E}_{n}
for
\mathcal{E}_{n}
the restriction of
\mathcal{E}
to
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{/A,n}
, the desired result follows as taking inverse limits commutes with taking cohomology and totalization.
∎

As a consequence of
Proposition

4.1.20
, we get that

Corollary 4.1.21
.

The global sections functor

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\bullet):\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})\quad\text{resp.}\quad\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{/A,n},\bullet):\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{/A,n})\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})

commutes with colimits.

Proposition 4.1.22
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
(resp.
\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}_{/A,n})
) is generated under shifts and colimits by the structure sheaf
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

As we work with the relative prismatization,
X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
lives over
A
, hence the ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}
is trivialized, i.e.
\mathcal{I}\cong\mathcal{O}
. Let
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
such that
\mathrm{RHom}(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{E})=0
, then
\mathrm{RHom}(\mathcal{I}^{n},\mathcal{E})=0
for all
n
as
\mathcal{I}^{n}\cong\mathcal{O}
. This implies that
\mathrm{RHom}(\mathcal{O}|_{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}},\mathcal{E}|_{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}})=0
, hence
\mathcal{E}|_{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}}=0
as
\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by the structure sheaf thanks to
[
AHLB23
, Lemma 6.12]
. Consequently
\mathcal{E}=0
.
∎

Theorem 4.1.23
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The functor

\displaystyle\beta_{n}^{+}:\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]),\qquad\mathcal{E}\mapsto(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E}),\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})

constructed in
Proposition

4.1.13
is fully faithful. Moreover, its essential image consists of those objects
M\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}])
satisfying the following pair of conditions:

•

M
is
(p,d)
-adically complete.

•

The action of
\nabla_{i}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes_{\tilde{R}/d^{n}}^{\mathbb{L}}\tilde{R}/(d,p))
is locally nilpotent for all
i
.

Proof.

The functor is well-defined thanks to
Proposition

4.1.13
. For the full faithfulness, let
\mathcal{E}
and
\mathcal{F}
be quasi-coherent complexes on
X_{/A,n}^{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}
and we want to show that the natural map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(X^{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{/A,n})}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}])}(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E}),\rho^{*}(\mathcal{F}))

is a homotopy equivalence. Thanks to
Proposition

4.1.22
, we could reduce to the case that
\mathcal{E}
is the structure sheaf. Then the desired result follows from
Proposition

4.1.20
and
Remark

4.1.12
.

By
Proposition

4.1.22
, the essential image of
\beta_{n}^{+}
is generated by
\tilde{R}/d^{n}
(with
\nabla_{i}
acting via
\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}
) under shifts and colimits, and
\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}
on
\tilde{R}/d^{n}
satisfies that
\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}(x)\in d(\tilde{R}/d^{n}),\forall x\in\tilde{R}/d^{n}
. In particular, the essential image of
\beta_{n}^{+}
is contained in the subcategory of
(p,d)
-complete complexes
M\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}])
such that each
\nabla_{i}
acts locally nilpotently on
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes_{\tilde{R}/d^{n}}^{\mathbb{L}}\tilde{R}/(d,p))
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}])
be the full subcategory spanned by objects satisfying two conditions listed in
Theorem

4.1.23
. As the source
\mathcal{D}(X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by the structure sheaf thanks to
Proposition

4.1.22
, to complete the proof it suffices to show that
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is also generated under shifts and colimits by
\beta^{+}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}})
. In other words, we need to show that for every nonzero object
M\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
,
M
admits a nonzero morphism from
\beta^{+}_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{X_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}})[m]
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Arguing as
Theorem

3.3.4
, we can reduce to the Hodge-Tate case and it suffices to show that for every nonzero object
M\in\mathcal{C}_{1}
,
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\beta_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{O}),M)\neq 0
. For this purpose, first we observe that
\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}(\beta_{1}^{+}(\mathcal{O}),M)
can be calculated by
D_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(M,\nabla)
, the
q
-de Rham complex of
M
thanks to
Remark

4.1.12
. Replacing
M
by
M\otimes k
(the derived Nakayama guarantees that
L\otimes k
detects whether
L
is zero or not for
p
-complete
L
), we may assume that there exists some cohomology group
\mathrm{H}^{-m}(M)
containing a nonzero element
x
killed by
\nabla_{i}^{N}
for all
i
, where
N
is fixed. Furthermore, we could assume this element is non-zero and is actually killed by
\nabla_{i}
for all
i
. Indeed, assume that
N
is the minimal positive integer such that
\nabla_{i}^{N}
kills
x
for all
i
. We do induction on
N
. If
N=1
, this is already done. Otherwise there exists
i
such that
\nabla_{i}^{N-1}(x)\neq 0
. we first replace
x
by
\nabla_{i}^{N-1}(x)
. If
x
is still not killed by
\nabla_{j}^{N-1}
, we replace
x
with
\nabla_{j}^{N-1}(x)
and then repeat the process until reduced to the case that
N=1
. Notice that in this step we crucially use the assumption that
\nabla_{i}
commutes with
\nabla_{j}
for all
i,j
. It then follows that
\mathrm{H}^{-m}(D_{{\mathrm{dR}}}(M,\nabla))\neq 0
, hence there exists a non-zero morphism from
\beta^{+}_{1}(\mathcal{O})/p[m]
to
M
as desired.
∎

4.2.
Locally complete intersection case

In this subsection we would like to extend the classification results in the previous section to the locally complete intersection case.

As in the last subsection, we still assume
X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)
is a small affine and fix the following diagram

\textstyle{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{\tilde{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bar{A}\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{R,}

where the horizontal maps are étale chart maps. But we allow
m=0
, for example,
R=\mathcal{O}_{K}
(and
\tilde{R}=A
) in this subsection.

In this paper we always consider
Y=\mathrm{Spf}(R/(\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{r}))\hookrightarrow X
(
x_{i}\in\tilde{R}
and
\bar{x}_{i}
is its image in
\bar{A}
) to be a closed embedding such that the prismatic envelope with respect to the morphism of
\delta
-pairs
(\tilde{R},(d))\to(\tilde{R},(d,x_{1},\cdots,x_{m}))
exists and is exactly given by
\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}
, obtained by freely adjoining
\frac{x_{i}}{d}
to
\tilde{R}
in the category of derived
(p,I)
-complete simplicial
\delta
-
A
-algebras
20
20

20

See
[
BS22
, Corollary 2.44]
for the precise definition.
.

We first verify that the assumption on
Y
holds for a large class of interesting objects.

Lemma 4.2.1
.

\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}
is discrete and
d
-torsion free (hence satisfies the above condition) in the following two cases:

•

(\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{r})
forms a
p
-completely regular sequence relative to
\mathcal{O}_{K}
in the sense of
[
BS22
, Definition 2.42]
.

•

x_{1}=(q-1)^{t}\in A
(
t\geq 1
),
(\bar{x}_{2},\cdots,\bar{x}_{r})
forms a
p
-completely regular sequence relative to
\mathcal{O}_{K}
.

Proof.

In the first situation, the desired result is
[
BS22
, Proposition 3.13]
as
(x_{1},\cdots,x_{r})
forms
(p,d)
-completely regular sequence relative to
A
. For the second case, it suffices to check for
r=1
and
x_{1}=(q-1)^{t}
. Without loss of generality, we can further assume
\tilde{R}=A
. To see
C=A\{\frac{(q-1)^{t}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}
is discrete and
d
-torsion free, by derived Nakayama it suffices to show that
C/^{\mathbb{L}}d
is discrete. Actually we will prove that
C/^{\mathbb{L}}d\simeq\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{G}_{a,\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{m}}^{\sharp}}
(here
\pi=q-1\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
).

For this purpose, we notice that by the proof of
Proposition

4.2.4
(which doesn’t require
C/^{\mathbb{L}}d
to be discrete),
\mathrm{Spf}(C/^{\mathbb{L}}d)
(in the derived sense) represents the functor sending a test
\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t}
-algebra
S
to
Y_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}(S)=\{y\in W(S)|~(q-1)^{t}=yd\}
(here
Y=\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t})
). Hence it suffices to show the latter is represented by
\mathbb{G}_{a,\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{m}}^{\sharp}
.

First we construct a base point in
\mathrm{Spf}(C/^{\mathbb{L}}d)(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t})=Y_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t})
. By definition (see
[
BS22
, Corollary 2.44]
),
C
is obtained from the pushout diagram

\textstyle{A\{x\}_{\delta}^{\wedge}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{w:x\mapsto(q-1)^{t}}
\scriptstyle{v:x\mapsto dz}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\{z\}_{\delta}^{\wedge}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}

in the
\infty
-category of simplicial commutative
\delta
-rings. Let
\iota:A\to A/(d,(q-1)^{t})=\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{m}
be the usual quotient map. Then it can be extended to a ring homomorphism
g:A\{z\}_{\delta}^{\wedge}\to\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{m}
by sending
\delta^{i}(z)
to
0
for all
i\geq 0
. Let
\tilde{g}:A\{z\}_{\delta}^{\wedge}\to W(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{m})
(resp.
\tilde{\iota}:A\to W(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{m})
) be the unique
\delta
-ring homomorphism lifting
g
(resp.
\iota
).

Let
a=q-1\in A=W(k)[[q-1]]
. Then
\varphi(a)=(a+1)^{p}-1
, hence

\delta(a)=\frac{\varphi(a)-a^{p}}{p}=\frac{(a+1)^{p}-a^{p}-1}{p}\in(a).

As
\delta(xy)=x^{p}\delta(y)+y^{p}\delta(x)+p\delta(x)\delta(y)
for
x,y\in A
, by induction we see that
\delta(a^{n})\in(a^{n}),\forall n\geq 1
. Indeed, a stronger statement that
\delta^{k}(a^{n})\in(a^{n}),\forall k\geq 1
also holds. We proceed by induction on
k
. Suppose that the claim holds up to
k
and let
c\in A
satisfies that
\delta^{k}(a^{n})=a^{n}c
, then

\delta^{k+1}(a^{n})=\delta(\delta^{k}(a^{n}))=\delta(a^{n}c)=\frac{\varphi(a^{n}c)-a^{np}c^{p}}{p}=\frac{((a+1)^{p}-1)^{n}\varphi(c)-a^{np}c^{p}}{p}=a^{np}\delta(c)+(a^{n})\in(a^{n}),

hence
\delta^{k+1}(a^{n})\in(a^{n})
and we finish the induction.

The above analysis shows that
\iota\circ w:A\{x\}_{\delta}^{\wedge}\to\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t}
kills all of
\delta^{i}(x)
,
i\geq 0
, which implies
\tilde{\iota}\circ w:A\{x\}_{\delta}^{\wedge}\to W(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t})
also kills all of
\delta^{i}(x)
,
i\geq 0
(by the uniqueness of
\delta
-lifting of
\iota\circ w
). Consequently
\tilde{l}((q-1)^{t})=0
and we get a base point
y=0\in Y_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t})
.

Finally we construct a desired isomorphism between functors
Y_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}
and
\mathbb{G}_{a,\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{m}}^{\sharp}
over
\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t})
. By
Lemma

4.2.2
, there exists a unit
x_{d}\in W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
such that
d=V(F(x_{d}))
holds in
W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
. Guaranteeing the existence of such
x_{d}
, for a test
\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t}
-algebra
S
, sending
y\in\mathbb{G}_{a,\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{m}}^{\sharp}(S)\simeq W(S)^{F=0}
to
y\in Y_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}(S)
defines an isomorphism of functors. The element
y
satisfies
dy=0
because
dy=V(F(x_{d}))\cdot y=V(F(x_{d})\cdot F(y))=V(0)=0
.
∎

The following lemma is used in the above proof.

Lemma 4.2.2
.

Let
\iota:A=W(k)[[q-1]]\to W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
be the unique
\delta
-ring map lifting the quotient map
A\to\mathcal{O}_{K}=A/([p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}})
. Then there exists a unit
x_{d}\in W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
such that
\iota(d)=V(F(x_{d}))
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we will just write
d
for
\iota(d)=\iota([p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}})
in the following proof. First notice that
d
maps to
0
under the projection
W(\mathcal{O}_{K})\to\mathcal{O}_{K}
, hence it lies in
V(W(\mathcal{O}_{K}))
. It then suffices to show that
\lambda=V(F(x))
has a solution
x_{d}=(x_{0},x_{1},\cdots)
in
W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
. As
\mathcal{O}_{K}
is
p
-torsion free, the ghost map is injective, hence this equation is equivalent to that

(4.4)

\displaystyle\forall n\geq 0,w_{n}(d)=w_{n}(V(F(x))).

We will construct
x_{d}=(x_{0},x_{1},\cdots)
inductively on
n
by showing that the solution exists in
W_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{K})
.

For
n=0
,
w_{0}(d)=0\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
,
w_{0}(V(F(x_{d})))=0
, hence
Eq.

4.4
always holds.

For
n\geq 1
, we have that

w_{n}(d)=w_{0}(\varphi^{n}(d))=w_{0}([p]_{q^{p^{\alpha+n}}})=p,

and that

w_{n}(V(F(x)))=pw_{n-1}(F(x))=pw_{n}(x)=p(\sum_{i=0}^{n}x_{i}^{p^{n-i}}p^{i}).

This implies if we take
x_{0}=1
and
x_{i}=0
for
i\geq 1
then
Eq.

4.4
holds for all
n
, i.e.
d=V(1)
in
W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
. Clearly such
x_{d}
is a unit in
W(\mathcal{O}_{K})
as
x_{0}=1
by construction.
∎

Remark 4.2.3
.

The main reason for considering the second case in
Lemma

4.2.1
is that we are interested in classifying quasi-coherent complexes on the prismatization of
Y=\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t})
later. Indeed, calculating the cohomology of the structure sheaf is already interesting and it (as well as its filtered versions) plays an important role in studying the
K
-thory of
\mathcal{O}_{K}/\pi^{t}
, see
[
AKN24
]
and
[
AKN23
]
for this picture.

Our goal is to study
\mathcal{D}(Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, where
Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
is the relative prismatization of
Y
with respect to
(A,I)
and hence fits into the following diagram

\textstyle{Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\bar{A})^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}}

where all squares in the diagram are pullback squares.

Our strategy is to use the covering map
Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\to Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
in the above diagram, which will still be denoted as
\rho
. Indeed,
Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
is nothing but
\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta})
, explained by the next proposition. This result is not surprising as
Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
could be viewed as the affine stack of
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(Y/\tilde{R},\mathcal{O}_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, which is precisely the prismatic envelope by the universal property of the later stated in
[
BS22
, Proposition 3.13]
.

Proposition 4.2.4
.

Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
(resp.
Y_{/\tilde{R},n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
) is represented by
\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta})
(resp.
\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}/d^{n})
).

Proof.

We only prove the statement for
Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
as the argument for
Y_{/\tilde{R},n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
is similar. For a
p
-nilpotent test algebra
S
over
\tilde{R}
with structure morphism
f:\tilde{R}\to S
, by definition we have that

Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(S)=\mathrm{Map}_{\tilde{R}}(R/(\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{r}),W(S)/{}^{\mathbb{L}}d),

where the mapping space is calculated in
p
-complete animated rings and
W(S)
is viewed as an algebra over
\tilde{R}
by lifting
f
to the unique
\delta
-ring homomorphism
\tilde{f}:\tilde{R}\to W(S)
. As the animated ring
R/(\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{r})
(resp.
W(S)/{}^{\mathbb{L}}d
) is obtained from
\tilde{R}
(resp.
W(S)
) by freely setting
(d,x_{1},\cdots,x_{r})
(resp.
d
) to be zero and that
\tilde{R}
is the initial object in the category of testing algebras, the above then simplifies to

Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(S)=\{(y_{1},\cdots,y_{r})\in W(S)^{r}|~x_{i}=y_{i}d,~1\leq i\leq r\}.

Given any
y=(y_{1},\cdots,y_{r})\in Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(S)
, the unique
\delta
-ring map
\tilde{f}:\tilde{R}\to W(S)
lifting the structure map
f:\tilde{R}\to S
extends uniquely to a
\delta
-ring map
\tilde{f}_{y}:\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}\to W(S)
by sending
\delta^{i}(\frac{x_{j}}{d})
to
\delta^{i}(y_{j})
(
i\geq 0,1\leq j\leq r
) due to the universal property of
\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}
. Considering the composition of the projection
W(S)\to S
and
\tilde{f}_{y}
, we obtain a ring homomorphism
f_{y}:\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}\to S
, corresponding to a point
f_{y}\in\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta})
.

Conversely, given a morphism
f:\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}\to S
,
f
uniquely lifts to a
\delta
-ring morphism
\tilde{f}:\mathfrak{S}\{\frac{p^{m}}{\lambda}\}_{\delta}^{\wedge}\to W(S)
by the universal property of Witt rings. Let
y_{f,j}=\tilde{f}(\frac{x_{j}}{d})
for
1\leq j\leq r
, then it satisfies that
x_{j}=y_{f,j}d
, hence
y_{f}:=(y_{f,1},\cdots,y_{f,r})
determines a point in
Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(S)
.

To see
y_{f_{y}}=y
, we just need to notice that given
y\in Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(S)
, the
\tilde{f}_{y}:\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}\to W(S)
constructed above is precisely the
\delta
-ring morphism lifting
f_{y}:\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}\to S
by construction.

Finally, for the purpose of showing that
f_{y_{f}}=f
, it suffices to observe that given
f:\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}\to S
,
\tilde{f}=\tilde{f}_{y_{f}}
by our construction. Then we are done.
∎

To ease the notation, from now on we denote
\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta}
as
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
. Given
Proposition

4.2.4
, we have the following pullback square

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))=\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R}\{\frac{x_{1},\cdots,x_{r}}{d}\}^{\wedge}_{\delta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X_{A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},}

hence we could apply the strategy in the previous subsection to study
\mathcal{D}(Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
. As usual, we construct
\psi
first.

Lemma 4.2.5
.

Let
S=\tilde{R}\oplus\epsilon\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}
be as that in
Lemma

4.1.1
. Then the
A
-linear homomorphism
\psi:\tilde{R}\to S
constructed in
Lemma

4.1.1
uniquely extends to a
\delta
-ring homomorphism

{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\to{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S,

which will still be denoted as
\psi
by abuse of notation. Moreover, this further induces an
\psi:\tilde{R}/(d,x_{1},\cdots,x_{r})^{n}\to{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S
after modulo
d^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

Arguing as that in
Remark

4.1.3
,
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S
could be promoted to a
\delta
-ring extending the
\delta
-structure on
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
. Moreover, as

\psi(x_{j})=x_{j}+\epsilon\nabla_{q}(x_{j})=x_{j}+\epsilon\cdot d\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(x_{j})-x_{j}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i},\quad\forall 1\leq j\leq r

we have the following holds in
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S
:

\frac{\psi(x_{j})}{d}=\frac{x_{j}}{d}+\epsilon\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(x_{j})-x_{j}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i}.

Hence
\psi:\tilde{R}\to S
(which is a
\delta
-ring homomorphism thanks to
Proposition

4.1.4
) extends to a unique
\delta
-ring homomorphism
\psi:{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\to{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S
sending
\frac{x_{j}}{d}
to
\frac{x_{j}}{d}+\epsilon\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(x_{j})-x_{j}}{q^{p}T_{i}-T_{i}}dT_{i}
by the universal property of
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
. For the moreover part, just notice that
\psi
preserves the
d
-adic filtration and
\psi(x_{j})\in(d)({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)
.
∎

Remark 4.2.6
.
