The same argument shows that
\psi_{i}
constructed in
Remark

4.1.2
also extends to
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
. Consequently,
\nabla_{i}=\frac{\psi_{i}-\mathrm{Id}}{\epsilon_{i}}
extends to
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
as well. By defining
\gamma_{i}=\mathrm{Id}+T_{i}\beta\nabla_{i}
, we get an extension of
\gamma_{i}
to
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
, which is still an automorphism of
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
. Moreover, the relations that
\nabla_{i}\circ\nabla_{j}=\nabla_{j}\circ\nabla_{i}
and that
\gamma_{i}\circ\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{j}\circ\gamma_{i}
still hold.

Warning 4.2.7
.

A key difference with the smooth case studied in the last subsection is that
\nabla_{i}
constructed in the last remark doesn’t need to vanish on the Hodge-Tate locus
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d
(for example,
\nabla_{i}(\frac{x_{i}}{d})
is not necessarily in
(d){{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
). This implies that although
\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}})
can be realized as modules over the commutative ring
R[\underline{\nabla}]
, in general this doesn’t hold for
Y
. Instead, certain non-commutative
Weyl-algebras
(which is a special kind of Ore extension) will always enter the picture for the purpose of classifying
\mathcal{D}(Y_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}})
. This phenomenon already appears for the Hodge-Tate stack of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}/p^{n}
, see
[
Pet23
, Lemma 6.13]
and
[
Liu24
, Theorem 6.9, Remark 6.12]
for details.

Given
Lemma

4.2.5
, we can restrict
Proposition

4.1.7
to
Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
as follows.

Proposition 4.2.8
.

\gamma_{c_{\psi}}
constructed in
Proposition

4.1.7
restricts to an isomorphism
\gamma_{c_{\psi}}
between functors
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))\xrightarrow{\rho}Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}

and
\rho\circ\psi:\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))\rightarrow Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
, i.e. we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{c_{\psi}}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{Y_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}}

Proof.

By abuse of notation we write
\iota:\tilde{R}\to{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S
for the composition of
\tilde{R}\to{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\xrightarrow{\iota}{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S
, then
\rho({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)
corresponds to the point

(\alpha:(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)\to W({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S),\eta:\Cone((d,x_{1},\cdots,x_{r})\to\tilde{R})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\iota}}\Cone(\alpha))

in
X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}(S)
, while
(\rho\circ\psi)(S)
corresponds to the point

(\alpha:(d)\otimes_{A,\tilde{\iota}}W({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)\to W({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S),\eta^{\prime}:\Cone((d,x_{1},\cdots,x_{r})\to\tilde{R})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\psi}}\Cone(\alpha)).

Arguing as
Lemma

4.1.6
, we deduce that
W({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)
is
d
-torsion free, hence the source of
\alpha
is just the ideal generated by
d
in
W({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)
.
By applying the last statement in
Lemma

4.2.5
and drawing the following diagram as maps of quasi-ideals (in the sense of
[
Dri20
]
),

\textstyle{(d,x_{1},\cdots,x_{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\iota}}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{\tilde{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\iota}}
\textstyle{(d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{W({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S).}

we derive that
c_{\psi}
constructed in
Proposition

4.1.5
serves a homotopy between
\eta^{\prime}
and
\eta
, hence finish the proof.
∎

Then following the discussion between
Proposition

4.1.8
and
Proposition

4.1.13
by taking
Proposition

4.2.8
as the input replacing
Proposition

4.1.7
, we obtain the following analog of
Proposition

4.1.13
:

Proposition 4.2.9
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the pullback along
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n})\to Y_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
induces a functor

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(Y_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})&\to\mathcal{D}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)},\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}])\\
\mathcal{E}&\mapsto(\rho^{*}\mathcal{E},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})\end{split}

which will be denoted as
\beta_{n}^{+}
later.

Remark 4.2.10
.

The non-commutative ring
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)},\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}]
stated in the last proposition is defined similarly to
Definition

4.1.11
. More precisely, let
\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}:{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\to{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
be the ring automorphism
\gamma_{i}
defined in
Remark

4.2.6
extending
\gamma_{\tilde{R},i}
on
\tilde{R}
, then
\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}:{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\to{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
, denoted as
\nabla_{i}
in
Remark

4.2.6
, is a
\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}
-derivation of
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)
, i.e.
\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}(x_{1}x_{2})=\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}(x_{1})\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}(x_{2})+\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}(x_{1})x_{2}
. We define the
Ore extension

{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)},\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}]
to be the noncommutative ring obtained by giving the ring of polynomials
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)[\nabla_{1},\cdots,\nabla_{m}]
a new multiplication law, subject to the identity

\begin{split}\nabla_{i}\cdot\nabla_{j}&=\nabla_{j}\cdot\nabla_{i},~~~\forall~1\leq i,j\leq m\\
\nabla_{i}\cdot r&=\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}(r)\cdot\nabla_{i}+\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}(r),~~~\forall~r\in{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),1\leq i\leq m.\end{split}

Then finally we can state our classification results for relative prismatic crystals on
(Y/A)_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
, generalizing
Theorem

4.1.23
to the locally complete intersection case.

Theorem 4.2.11
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The functor

\displaystyle\beta_{n}^{+}:\mathcal{D}(Y_{/A,n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)},\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}]),\qquad\mathcal{E}\mapsto(\rho^{*}(\mathcal{E}),\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})

constructed in
Proposition

4.2.9
is fully faithful. Moreover, its essential image consists of those objects
M\in\mathcal{D}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)},\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}])
satisfying the following pair of conditions:

•

M
is
(p,d)
-adically complete.

•

The action of
\nabla_{i}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}}^{\mathbb{L}}{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/(d,p))
is locally nilpotent for all
i
.

Proof.

Given
Proposition

4.2.9
, the strategy we employed proving
Theorem

4.1.23
still works once we show that the theorem holds for
n=1
, i.e. the Hodge-Tate case.

For this purpose, first we notice that
\rho:X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)\to X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}
is a cover with automorphism group
T_{X/\bar{A}}^{\sharp}\{1\}\cong(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}
(see
[
BL22b
, Proposition 5.12]
and discussion after
Proposition

4.1.14
, here
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}
is defined over
X
), hence by restricting the pullback square above
Lemma

4.2.5
to the Hodge-Tate locus, we obtain the following pullback diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}=\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d)/(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}=X/(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}=\mathrm{Spf}(R)/(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}.}

Here
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}_{Y}
is the base change of
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}
along the closed embedding
Y\to X
.

Consequently, we have that

(4.5)

\mathcal{D}(Y_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}})=\mathrm{Mod}_{\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{D}(X/(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}))=\mathrm{Mod}_{\beta_{1}^{+}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O})}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}(R[\underline{\nabla}])).

Here the first identity holds as
\pi
is affine, and the second equality follows from
Theorem

4.1.23
by taking
n=1
there. Also, we use
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}(R[\underline{\nabla}])
22
22

22

Notice that when
n=1
,
\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
is commutative and is just the free polynomial over
R
with
m
-variables
\nabla_{i}(1\leq i\leq m)
, hence we just write
R[\underline{\nabla}]
for simplicity.
to denote the essential image of
\beta_{1}^{+}
stated in
Theorem

4.1.23
for simplicity.

But we claim that the right-hand side of
Eq.

4.5
is exactly the category stated in
Theorem

4.2.11
.

For this purpose, we first notice that the underlying complex of
\beta_{1}^{+}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O})
is given by
\rho^{*}\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}=\pi^{\prime}_{*}\rho^{\prime*}\mathcal{O}={{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d
. Moreover, unwinding the identification of
Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{\operatorname{HT}}(S)
with
\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d)
in
Proposition

4.2.4
as well as the identification of
\mathrm{Aut}(\rho)
with
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}
, we see that the
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}
-action on
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d
is given (hence is also determined) by the usual addition action on
\frac{x_{i}}{d}
, namely given a test algebra
Q
over
Y=\mathrm{Spf}(R/(\bar{x}_{1},\cdots,\bar{x}_{r}))
,

\begin{split}(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}(Q)\times Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{\operatorname{HT}}(Q)&\longrightarrow Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{\operatorname{HT}}(Q)\\
((t_{1},\cdots,t_{r}),(y_{1},\cdots,y_{r}))&\longmapsto(t_{1}+y_{1},\cdots,t_{r}+y_{r}).\end{split}

This implies that the
q
-Higgs derivation associated with
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}
via
Theorem

4.1.23
is precisely
\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}
described in
Remark

4.2.10
. Consequently, we see that

\beta_{1}^{+}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O})=({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d,\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)})\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}(R[\underline{\nabla}]).

Finally, we notice that there is a canonical tensor structure on
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}(R[\underline{\nabla}])
inherited from that on
\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
via
Theorem

4.1.23
. In particular, for
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}(R[\underline{\nabla}])
, the underlying complex for
\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{F}
is
\mathcal{E}\otimes_{R}\mathcal{F}
and
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{F},i}
is given by
\nabla_{\mathscr{E},i}\otimes\mathrm{Id}_{\mathscr{E}^{\prime}}+\mathrm{Id}_{\mathscr{E}}\otimes\nabla_{\mathscr{E}^{\prime},i}+\beta T_{i}\nabla_{\mathscr{E},i}\otimes\nabla_{\mathscr{E}^{\prime},i}

according to
Remark

4.1.10
. Consequently
\mathcal{H}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}(R[\underline{\nabla}])
is a
\beta_{1}^{+}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O})
-module if and only if the underlying complex of
\mathcal{H}
is a
{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d
-module and the
\underline{\nabla}
action on
\mathcal{H}
satisfies that

\nabla_{i}\cdot a=\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}(a)\cdot\nabla_{i}+\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y),i}(a),~~~\forall a\in{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d.

Such conditions exactly determine an object in
\mathcal{D}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)},\nabla_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}])
, hence we finish the proof.
∎

5.
On the absolute prismatization

5.1.
Smooth case

In this section we work with
\mathcal{O}_{K}=W(k)[\zeta_{p^{\alpha+1}}]
(
\alpha\geq 0
) and the
q
-prism
(A,I)=(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}})
.
We consider
X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)
, a smooth
p
-adic formal scheme over
\overline{A}=\mathcal{O}_{K}
. As in the last section, we assume that
X
is a small affine and fix the following diagram

\textstyle{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{\tilde{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bar{A}\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{R,}

where the horizontal maps are étale chart maps.
By deformation theory, this étale chart map uniquely lifts to a prism
(\tilde{R},I)
over
(A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle,I)
, here
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
is equipped with the
\delta
structure respecting that on
A
and sending
T_{i}
to
0
, then we apply
[
BS21
, Lemma 2.18]
to uniquely extend such a
\delta
-structure to
\tilde{R}
. Moreover, the induced map
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\to\tilde{R}
is
(p,I)
-completely étale. In particular,
(\tilde{R},I)
is a prism in
(X/A)_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
and
\tilde{R}/I=R
. We have the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{A}}
\textstyle{X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},}

here the square is a pullback square and we use
\rho_{A}
to denote the covering map,
\rho_{X}=\rho_{A}\circ\rho
.

In Section 3, we studied the difference between
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(A))
and
\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, which turns out to be governed by an operator
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
satisfying certain twisted Leibnitz rule for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
by
Theorem

3.3.4
. Then later in Section 4, we equipped a
q
-Higgs derivation
\nabla_{\mathcal{F}}
on
\rho^{*}\mathcal{F}
for
\mathcal{F}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, which is enough for the purpose of classify
\mathcal{D}(X_{/A}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
. With such results in hand, it is natural to expect that for
\mathcal{G}\in\mathcal{D}({X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}})
,
\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{G}
is equipped with both a
q
-connection
\partial_{\mathcal{F}}:\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{G}
and a
q
-Higgs derivation
\nabla_{\mathcal{G}}:\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{G}\otimes_{\tilde{R}}\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}
. Moreover, we should be able to fully understand
\mathcal{D}({X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}})
with the help of
\partial
and
\nabla
. We aim to show this is indeed the case in this section.

Per
Remark

4.0.1
, in this section
\beta=q^{p^{\alpha}}-1
. For any
i\in\{1,\cdots,m\}
, we define the automorphism
\gamma_{i}
of
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
fixing
A
and sending
T_{i}
to
q^{p^{\alpha+1}}T_{i}
and
T_{j}
to
T_{j}
for
j\neq i
. We further define an automorphism
\gamma_{0}
of
A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle
fixing
T_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq m
) and sending
q
to
q^{p^{\alpha+1}}\cdot q=q^{p^{\alpha+1}+1}
. Then these automorphisms lift uniquely to automorphisms
\gamma_{i}
of
\tilde{R}
following the discussions above
Lemma

4.1.1
. Moreover, one can check by hand that they satisfy the following:

(5.1)

\gamma_{i}\circ\gamma_{j}=\gamma_{j}\circ\gamma_{i},~~1\leq i,j\leq m,\quad\gamma_{0}\circ\gamma_{i}=\gamma_{i}^{p^{\alpha+1}+1}\gamma_{0},1\leq i\leq m.

Following the notation in the last section (above
Lemma

4.1.1
), we define
\partial
and
\nabla_{i}
on
\tilde{R}
via

(5.2)

\partial(f)=d\frac{\gamma_{0}(f)-f}{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}\cdot q-q}=\frac{\gamma_{0}(f)-f}{q\beta},\quad\nabla_{i}(f)=d\frac{\gamma_{i}(f)-f}{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}T_{i}-T_{i}}=\frac{\gamma_{i}(f)-f}{\beta T_{i}},\quad\nabla(f)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla_{i}(f)\epsilon_{i}.

As a consequence of
Eq.

5.1
,

(5.3)

\begin{split}\nabla_{i}\circ\nabla_{j}&=\nabla_{j}\circ\nabla_{i}\quad 1\leq i,j\leq m,\\
(\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial)\circ(\mathrm{Id}+T_{i}\beta\nabla_{i})&=(\mathrm{Id}+T_{i}\beta\nabla_{i})^{p^{\alpha+1}+1}\circ(\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial)\quad 1\leq i\leq m.\end{split}

Equation
5.3
could then be reinterpreted as the following lemma, which will be used later.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

\forall~1\leq i\leq m
,

(1+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i}))\nabla_{i}\circ\partial=s_{0}(\partial-s_{0}^{-1}\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i})+s_{1})\circ\nabla_{i},

where
s_{0}=\frac{\gamma_{0}(\beta)}{\beta[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}\in A^{\times}
and
s_{1}=q^{-1}\frac{(1+q\partial(\beta))-[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}{\gamma_{0}(\beta)}
for
k=p^{\alpha+1}+1
,
\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i})
is defined in the proof.

Proof.

For ease of notation, we omit
i
and write
\nabla_{i}
as
\nabla
in the proof. First we show that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists a sequence
\{a_{k,j}\}_{1\leq j\leq k}
such that

(\mathrm{Id}+T\beta\nabla)^{k}=\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{k,j}(T\beta\nabla)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}p^{\alpha+1}}

and that

a_{k,1}=[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}},\quad a_{k+1,j}=a_{k,j}(1+\beta d[j]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}})+a_{k,j-1},\quad j\geq 1,\text{here we define}~~a_{k,0}=1.

We do induction on
k
. Clearly it holds when
k=1
. Suppose the claim holds up to
k
. By invoking
Lemma

4.1.1
and noticing that
\nabla(T)=d,\nabla(T^{j})=T^{j-1}d[j]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}
, hence

\begin{split}(\mathrm{Id}+T\beta\nabla)^{k+1}&=(\mathrm{Id}+T\beta\nabla)\circ(\mathrm{Id}+T\beta\nabla)^{k}=(\mathrm{Id}+T\beta\nabla)\circ(\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{k,j}(T\beta\nabla)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}p^{\alpha+1}})\\
&=\mathrm{Id}+T\beta\nabla+\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}a_{k,j}(T^{j}\beta^{j}\nabla^{j}+\beta^{j+1}T\nabla\circ(T^{j}\nabla^{j}))q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}p^{\alpha+1}}\\
&=\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}(a_{k,j}T\beta\nabla^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}p^{\alpha+1}}(1+\beta d[j]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}})+a_{k,j-1}\beta^{j}Tq^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}p^{\alpha+1}}\cdot q^{p^{\alpha+1}(j-1)}T^{j-1}\nabla^{j})\\
&=\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}(a_{k,j}(1+\beta d[j]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}})+a_{k,j-1})(T\beta\nabla)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}p^{\alpha+1}}.\end{split}

This implies the desired induction formula for
a_{k,j}
. It then follows that
a_{k,1}=[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}
by induction and observing that
\beta d=q^{p^{\alpha+1}}-1
.

From now we fix
k=p^{\alpha+1}+1
and rewite
a_{k,j}
just as
a_{j}
. Then the above calculation tells us that

\begin{split}(\mathrm{Id}+T\beta\nabla)^{p^{\alpha+1}+1}\circ(\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial)&=(\mathrm{Id}+[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}T\beta\nabla+\sum_{j=2}^{k}a_{j}(T\beta\nabla)^{j}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}p^{\alpha+1}})(\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial)\\
&=\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial+[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}(T\beta\nabla+qT\beta^{2}\nabla\circ\partial+qT\beta^{2}\mathscr{D}(\nabla)\nabla+q^{2}\beta^{3}T\mathscr{D}(\nabla)\nabla\partial),\end{split}

here we define
\mathscr{D}(\nabla)=\frac{1}{[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}\sum_{j=2}^{k}a_{j}T^{j-1}\beta^{j-2}\nabla^{j-1}q^{\frac{j(j-1)}{2}p^{\alpha+1}-1}
, which makes sense as
[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}\in\tilde{R}^{\times}
. Indeed, it projects to the unit
k=p^{\alpha+1}+1
after modulo
q-1
.

On the other hand, one calculates that

\begin{split}&(\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial)\circ(\mathrm{Id}+T\beta\nabla)=\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial+T\beta\nabla+qT\beta\partial(\beta\nabla)\\
=&\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial+T\beta(1+q\partial(\beta))\nabla+qT\beta\gamma_{0}(\beta)\partial\nabla.\end{split}

Notice that

\frac{\gamma_{0}(\beta)}{\beta}=\frac{q^{kp^{\alpha}}-1}{q^{p^{\alpha}}-1}=[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}}\in\tilde{R}^{\times}

as its projection after modulo
q-1
is given by
k=p^{\alpha+1}+1
, which is a unit.

Also,
[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}-(1+q\partial(\beta))=[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}-q^{p^{\alpha}}d[p^{\alpha}]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}-1
is divisible by
\beta
as its image after modulo
\beta=q^{p^{\alpha}}-1
is given by
k-1-p\cdot p^{\alpha}=0
.

Combining all of the calculations above, Equation
5.3
then states that

\begin{split}(\nabla+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla)\nabla)\circ\partial&=(\frac{\gamma_{0}(\beta)}{\beta[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}\partial-\mathscr{D}(\nabla)+q^{-1}\frac{(1+q\partial(\beta))-[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}{\beta[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}})\circ\nabla\\
&=s_{0}(\partial-s_{0}^{-1}\mathscr{D}(\nabla)+s_{1})\circ\nabla.\end{split}

for
s_{0}=\frac{\gamma_{0}(\beta)}{\beta[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}\in A^{\times}
and
s_{1}=q^{-1}\frac{(1+q\partial(\beta))-[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}{\gamma_{0}(\beta)}
.
∎

Remark 5.1.2
.

By considering the action of two sides of
Lemma

5.1.1
on
T_{i}\in\tilde{R}
, we see that
s_{1}=-\frac{\partial(d)}{d}\in A
.

The following lemma combines
Lemma

3.1.1
and
Lemma

4.1.1
. For simplicity, we rewrite
\epsilon dT_{i}
as
\epsilon_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
in this section.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Let
S=\tilde{R}\oplus_{i=0}^{m}\tilde{R}\epsilon_{i}
and we regulate the following algebra structure on
S
:

(\epsilon_{0})^{2}=\beta q\cdot\epsilon_{0},\quad(\epsilon_{i})^{2}=\beta T_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{i},~~1\leq i\leq m,\quad\epsilon_{i}\cdot\epsilon_{j}=0~~\text{for}~~i\neq j.

Then the map

\begin{split}\psi:\tilde{R}&\longrightarrow S\\
f&\longmapsto=f+d\frac{\gamma_{0}(f)-f}{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}\cdot q-q}\epsilon_{0}+d\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\gamma_{i}(f)-f}{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}T_{i}-T_{i}}\epsilon_{i}\end{split}

defines a ring homomorphism of
W(k)
-algebras. similarly, we get ring homomorphism
\psi_{i}
as that in
Remark

4.1.2
for
0\leq i\leq m
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

3.1.1
and
Lemma

4.1.1
after noticing that for
f=q^{k}
,

\begin{split}\frac{\gamma_{0}(f)-f}{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}\cdot q-q}=\frac{(q^{p^{\alpha+1}+1})^{k}-q^{k}}{q^{p^{\alpha+1}+1}-q}=q^{k-1}[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}},\end{split}

hence
\psi(q^{k})
coincides with that in
Lemma

3.1.1
.
∎

Proposition 5.1.4
.

The elements
b
and
c
constructed in
Proposition

3.1.5
and
Proposition

3.1.8
together with

constructed in

induce an isomorphism
\gamma_{b,c_{\psi}}
between functors
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}(S)\to\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\xrightarrow{\rho_{X}}X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}

and
\rho\circ\psi:\mathrm{Spf}(S)\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\xrightarrow{\rho_{X}}X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
, i.e. we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Proof.

We write
\iota:\tilde{R}\to S
for the canonical map as usual, then
\rho(S)
corresponds to the point

(\alpha:(d)\otimes_{\tilde{R},\tilde{\iota}}W(S)\to W(S),\eta:\Cone((d)\to\tilde{R})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\iota}}\Cone(\alpha))

in

, while

corresponds to the point

We need to specify an isomorphism
\gamma_{b}:\alpha^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\alpha
as well as a homotopy
\gamma_{c_{\psi}}
between
\gamma_{b}\circ\eta^{\prime}
and
\eta
.
\gamma_{b}
is constructed as that in

3.1.9
by viewing

constructed in

3.1.5
as an element of
W(S)
. To construct the homotopy, we just need to notice that for any
x\in S
, there exists a unique
c_{\psi}(x)
in
W(S)
such that
\tilde{\psi}_{(}x)-\tilde{\iota}(x)=d\cdot c_{\psi}(x)
by combining
Proposition

3.1.8
and
Proposition

4.1.5
, then the proof of
Proposition

3.1.9
still works here.
∎

Then following the discussion before
Proposition

4.1.8
, for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
,
\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}
is equipped with a
q

.

Proposition 5.1.5
.

For
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
,
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}\otimes dT_{i}
and
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
on
\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}
satisfies the following property:

\begin{split}\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}\wedge\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}&=0,\\
(\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial_{\mathcal{E}})\circ(\mathrm{Id}+T_{i}\beta\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i})&=(\mathrm{Id}+T_{i}\beta\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i})^{p^{\alpha+1}+1}\circ(\mathrm{Id}+q\beta\partial_{\mathcal{E}})\quad 1\leq i\leq m.\end{split}

Moreover,
\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}
and
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
satisfies the relation stated in
Lemma

5.1.1
, i.e.

(\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i})\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i})\circ\partial_{\mathcal{E}}=s_{0}(\partial_{\mathcal{E}}-s_{0}^{-1}\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i})+s_{1})\circ\nabla_{\mathcal{E},i}.

Proof.

The first sentence is due to
Proposition

4.1.8
. To prove the second statement, we may assume that

. Let

and
S_{1}=\tilde{R}[\epsilon_{1}]/(\epsilon_{1}^{2}-\beta T_{1}\epsilon_{0})=\tilde{R}\oplus\tilde{R}\cdot\epsilon_{1}
.
For

, we define
\psi_{i}:\tilde{R}\to S_{i}=\tilde{R}[\epsilon_{i}]
as that in
Remark

4.1.2
.

We consider the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{j_{1}:\epsilon_{1}\mapsto\beta T_{1}}

Playing the same game for
\gamma_{0}
and considering the following diagram

we see that the unique isomorphism (induced by

hence it must vanish, which finishes the proof.
∎

In the special case that
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})^{\heartsuit}
,
\rho_{X}^{*}(\mathscr{E})
is a
\tilde{R}
-module concentrated on degree

and

(resp.

) satisfies the twisted Leibnitz rule stated in

(resp.

).

Given
Proposition

5.1.5
, we introduce the following non-commutative ring, which will be used later for the purpose of classifying
\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
.

Definition 5.1.6
.

Let
\gamma_{\tilde{R},i}:\tilde{R}\to\tilde{R}
(
0\leq i\leq m
) be the ring automorphism
\gamma_{i}
defined above
Lemma

5.1.1
, then
\partial_{\tilde{R}}:\tilde{R}\to\tilde{R}
(resp.
\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}:\tilde{R}\to\tilde{R}
for
i\geq 1
), denoted as
\partial
(resp.
\nabla_{i}
) above
Lemma

5.1.1
, is a
\gamma_{\tilde{R},0}
-derivation (resp.
\gamma_{\tilde{R},i}
-derivation) of
\tilde{R}
. We define the
Ore extension

\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
to be the noncommutative ring obtained by giving the ring of polynomials
\tilde{R}[\partial,\nabla_{1},\cdots,\nabla_{m}]
a new multiplication law, subject to the identity

\begin{split}\nabla_{i}\cdot\nabla_{j}&=\nabla_{j}\cdot\nabla_{i},~~~\forall~1\leq i,j\leq m,\\
\nabla_{i}\cdot r&=\gamma_{\tilde{R},i}(r)\cdot\nabla_{i}+\nabla_{\tilde{R},i}(r),~~~\forall~r\in\tilde{R},1\leq i\leq m,\\
\partial\cdot r&=\gamma_{\tilde{R},0}(r)\cdot\partial+\partial_{\tilde{R}}(r),~~~\forall~r\in\tilde{R},\\
\partial\cdot\nabla_{i}&=s_{0}^{-1}(1+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i}))\nabla_{i}\cdot\partial+(s_{0}^{-1}\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i})-s_{1})\cdot\nabla_{i},~~~\forall~1\leq i\leq m.\end{split}

We observe that
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
(denoted as
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
in
Definition

4.1.11
) is isomorphic to the quotient of
\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
by the left ideal generated by
\partial
. Combining with
Remark

4.1.12
, we obtain the following:

Lemma 5.1.7
.

There is a canonical resolution of
\tilde{R}
by finite free
\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
-modules:

\textstyle{\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times\partial^{[m]}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq m}\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times\partial^{[m-1]}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{1\leq k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}\leq m}\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times\partial^{[m-s]}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\times\partial}
\textstyle{\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq m}\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{1\leq k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}\leq m}\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}],}

where
\bigoplus_{1\leq k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}\leq m}\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
in the first row (resp. the second row) lives in (cohomological) bi-degree
(s-m,-1)
(resp.
(s-m,0)
) and the horizontal differential in the second row (resp. the first row) from
\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
in spot
k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}
to
\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
in spot
p_{1}<\cdots<p_{s+1}
is nonzero only if
\{k_{1},\cdots,k_{s}\}\subseteq\{p_{1},\cdots,p_{s+1}\}
, in which case it sends
f\in\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
to
(-1)^{u-1}f\cdot\nabla_{p_{u}}
(resp.
(-1)^{u-1}f\cdot(1+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}}))\nabla_{p_{u}}
), where
u\in\{1,\cdots,s+1\}
is the unique integer such that
p_{u}\notin\{k_{1},\cdots,k_{s}\}
. The vertical map
\partial^{[m-s]}
is defined in the proof.

Proof.

Given
Remark

4.1.12
, it suffices to construct
\partial^{[t]}
to make the diagram commute and moreover, each vertical map gives a resolution for
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
. Let
P_{n}^{i}
be the elementary symmetric polynomials in
n
variables of degree
i
, i.e.
P_{n}^{i}((x_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n})=P_{n}^{i}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{n})=\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\cdots<j_{i}\leq n}\prod_{t=1}^{i}x_{j_{t}}
. Then we define
\partial^{[t]}
by sending
f\in\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
in spot
k_{1}<\cdots<k_{m-t}
to the following element in
\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
(in spot
k_{1}<\cdots<k_{m-t}
as well):

f\cdot(s_{0}^{t}\partial+(\sum_{i=1}^{t}s_{0}^{i})s_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{t}\beta^{i-1}q^{i-1}P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{1}}),\cdots,\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{t}}))),

where
s_{0},s_{1},\mathscr{D}
are defined in
Lemma

5.1.1
and
\{p_{1},\cdots p_{t}\}=\{1,\cdots,m\}\backslash\{k_{1},\cdots,k_{m-t}\}
. In particular, if
t=0
, then
\partial^{[0]}=\partial
. Clearly each vertical map
\partial^{[t]}
defined in this way gives a resolution of
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
as
s_{0}
is a unit in
A
. To verify that it really defines a morphism of complexes, it amounts to checking that for a fixed spot
k_{1}<\cdots<k_{m-t}
and any
p_{j}\in\{p_{1},\cdots p_{t}\}=\{1,\cdots,m\}\backslash\{k_{1},\cdots,k_{m-t}\}
,

(s_{0}^{t}\partial+(\sum_{i=1}^{t}(s_{0}^{i}s_{1}-(\beta q)^{i-1}P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}}))))\cdot\nabla_{p_{j}}=((1-\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{j}}))\nabla_{p_{j}})\cdot(s_{0}^{t-1}\partial+(\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}(s_{0}^{i}s_{1}-(\beta q)^{i-1}P^{i}_{t-1}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}}))))),

here for simplicity we write
P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}}))
for
P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{1}}),\cdots,\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{t}}))
and
P^{i}_{t-1}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}}))
for

P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{1}}),\cdots,\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{j-1}}),\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{j+1}}),\cdots\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{t}}))
. But this follows from the observation that

\begin{split}&((1+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{j}}))\nabla_{p_{j}})\cdot(s_{0}^{t-1}\partial+(\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}(s_{0}^{i}s_{1}-(\beta q)^{i-1}P^{i}_{t-1}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}})))))\\
=&(s_{0}^{t-1}(s_{0}\partial-\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{j}})+s_{0}s_{1})+(\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}s_{0}^{i})s_{1}+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{j}})(\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}s_{0}^{i})s_{1}+\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{j}})-\sum_{i=1}^{t}\beta^{i-1}q^{i-1}P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}})))\cdot\nabla_{p_{j}}\\
=&(s_{0}^{t}\partial+(\sum_{i=1}^{t}(s_{0}^{i}s_{1}-(\beta q)^{i-1}P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}}))))\cdot\nabla_{p_{j}}+(1-s_{0}^{t-1}+\beta q\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}s_{0}^{i}s_{1})\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{j}})\cdot\nabla_{p_{j}}\\
=&(s_{0}^{t}\partial+(\sum_{i=1}^{t}(s_{0}^{i}s_{1}-(\beta q)^{i-1}P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{p_{u}}))))\cdot\nabla_{p_{j}}.\end{split}

Here the first equality follows from
Lemma

5.1.1
and the commutativity of
\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{a})
and
\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{b})
for
a\neq b
and the last identify follows from the vanishing of
s_{0}(1-s_{1}\beta q)-1
:

\begin{split}s_{0}(1-s_{1}\beta q)=\frac{\gamma_{0}(\beta)}{\beta[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}(1-\beta q\cdot q^{-1}\frac{(1+q\partial(\beta))-[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}{\gamma_{0}(\beta)})=\frac{\frac{\gamma_{0}(\beta)}{\beta}-(1+q\partial(\beta))+[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}{[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}=1,\end{split}

where the last identity holds as
\gamma_{0}(\beta)=\beta+q\beta\partial(\beta)
.
∎

As a quick corollary, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 5.1.8
.

Let
N
be an object in
\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])
, the derived category of (left)
\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}},\nabla_{\tilde{R}}]
-modules. If we further assume that
N
is derived
(p,d)
-complete, then

\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])}(\tilde{R},N)\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\partial}^{[\bullet]}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla)),

where
\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla)
is the
q
-de Rham complex of
N
defined in
Remark

4.1.12
and
\tilde{\partial}^{[t]}
acts on
N
sitting in spot
l_{1}<\cdots<l_{t}
via

(\prod_{i=1}^{t}(1+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{l_{i}}))^{-1})(s_{0}^{t}\partial+(\sum_{i=1}^{t}s_{0}^{i})s_{1}-\sum_{i=1}^{t}\beta^{i-1}q^{i-1}P^{i}_{t}(\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{l_{1}}),\cdots,\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{l_{t}}))).

Proof.

For any
N\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])
,
Lemma

5.1.7
implies that

\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])}(\tilde{R},N)\simeq\operatorname{fib}(\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial^{[\bullet]}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla^{\prime}))

with
\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla^{\prime}):=[N\xrightarrow{\nabla_{N}^{\prime}:=\sum(1+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i}))\nabla_{i}\otimes dT_{i}}N\otimes_{\tilde{R}}\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}\xrightarrow{\nabla^{\prime}_{N}\wedge\nabla^{\prime}_{N}}\cdots\to N\otimes_{\tilde{R}}\Omega_{\tilde{R}/A}^{m}\to 0]
.

If
N
is further
(p,d)
-complete, then
1+\beta q\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i})
is invertible on
N
(as
\beta
is topologically nilpotent with respect to the
(p,d)
-topology), hence we could replace
\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla^{\prime})
(resp.
\partial^{[\bullet]}
) with
\mathrm{DR}(N,\nabla)
(resp.
\tilde{\partial}^{[\bullet]}
) to make the diagram commute.
∎

Remark 5.1.9
.

Heuristically one might want to view an object in
\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])
as an “enhanced
q
-Higgs module", where such a notation is motivated by
[
MW22
, Definition 4.1]
24
24

24

Notice that
d^{\prime}(q)
in our setting plays the role of
E^{\prime}(\pi)
in
[
MW22
, Definition 4.1]
and the following reinterpretation of the last condition in
Definition

5.1.6
when
d=0
, which is a twisted version of the requirement that
\phi\circ\theta-\theta\circ\phi=E^{\prime}(\pi)\theta
given in
[
MW22
, Definition 4.1]
.

Lemma 5.1.10
.

If we work with
\tilde{R}/d[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
instead, then the last condition in
Definition

5.1.6
is equivalent to that

(1+q\beta\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i}))\nabla_{i}\cdot\partial=(\frac{1}{p^{\alpha+1}+1}\partial-\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i})-\frac{e}{p^{\alpha+1}+1})\cdot\nabla_{i}

for which
e=d^{\prime}(q)\in\mathcal{O}_{K}
and
\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i})=\frac{1}{q(p^{\alpha+1}+1)}\sum_{j=2}^{p^{\alpha+1}+1}\binom{p^{\alpha+1}+1}{j}T_{i}^{j-1}\beta^{j-2}\nabla_{i}^{j-1}
.

Proof.

First we notice that after modulo
d
,
q^{p^{\alpha+1}}
is identified with
1
, hence the inductive formula for
a_{k,j}
in
Lemma

5.1.1
turns into that
a_{k+1,j}=a_{k,j}+a_{k,j-1}
with
a_{k,1}=k
, hence
a_{k,j}=\binom{k}{j}
. Applying it to
k=p^{\alpha+1}+1
, we get the desired formula for
\mathscr{D}(\nabla_{i})
. Then by
Lemma

3.2.1
,
eq\beta=p^{\alpha+1}
(be aware that
\beta
in
Lemma

3.2.1
is
q\beta
here) in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
, hence

q^{-1}\frac{(1+q\partial(\beta))-[k]_{q^{p^{\alpha+1}}}}{\beta})=\frac{1-(p^{\alpha+1}+1)}{q\beta}=-e.

The desired formula then follows by
Lemma

5.1.1
.
∎

Remark 5.1.11
.

The above calculation implies that if we further modulo
q-1
, i.e. working with
\tilde{R}/(p,d)[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]
, then the above relation reduces to that

\nabla\cdot\partial=\partial\cdot\nabla

as
\beta=0
and
\binom{p^{\alpha+1}+1}{2}=0
in
A/(d,q-1)
, which implies the vanishing of
\mathscr{D}(\nabla)
.

Summarizing the discussion so far, we get the following analog of
Proposition

4.1.13
.

Proposition 5.1.12
.

For
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the pullback along
\rho_{X}:\mathrm{Spf}(\tilde{R})\to X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
induces a functor

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})&\to\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])\\
\mathcal{E}&\mapsto(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\partial_{\mathcal{E}},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}),\end{split}

which will be denoted as
\beta_{n}^{+}
later.

By combining
Proposition

3.2.3
and
Proposition

4.1.17
, we get the following result, which is a combination of
Proposition

4.1.20
and
Proposition

3.3.1
.

Proposition 5.1.13
.

Assume that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. For any
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
, the pullback functor
\beta_{n}^{+}:\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\to\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])
constructed in
Proposition

5.1.12
is fully faithful. Consequently,

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}},\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{fib}(\mathrm{DR}(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\partial}_{\mathcal{E}}^{[\bullet]}}\mathrm{DR}(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})),

where
\tilde{\partial}_{\mathcal{E}}^{[\bullet]}
is constructed in
Corollary

5.1.8
.

Proof.

Arguing as that in
Proposition

4.1.20
, by standard dévissage, it suffices to prove the statement for
n=1
. By restricting the diagram shown up at the beginning of this section to the Hodge-Tate locus, we get the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{X}}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\textstyle{X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{A}}
\textstyle{X^{\operatorname{HT}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{A}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\operatorname{HT}},}

where
f
is the structure morphism. Moreover,
f
is affine by
[
AHLB23
, Lemma 2.10]
, hence
f_{*}=Rf_{*}
. Consequently, for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
,

(5.4)

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Spf}(\mathcal{O}_{K})^{\operatorname{HT}},f_{*}\mathcal{E})\cong\operatorname{fib}(\rho_{A}^{*}f_{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\partial_{f_{{}_{*}}\mathcal{E}}}\rho_{A}^{*}f_{*}\mathcal{E}),

where the last equality is due to
Proposition

3.2.3
. On the other hand, we notice that

\rho_{A}^{*}f_{*}\mathcal{E}\cong f^{\prime}_{*}\rho_{A}^{*}\mathcal{E}=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X_{/A}^{\operatorname{HT}},\rho_{A}^{*}\mathcal{E})\cong\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}[\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])}(\tilde{R},\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E})\cong\mathrm{DR}(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}).

Here the second to last isomorphism follows from
Theorem

4.1.23
and the last isomorphism is due to
Remark

4.1.12
. Moreover, by the proof of
Corollary

5.1.8
and unwinding the construction of
\partial_{f_{{}_{*}}\mathcal{E}}
and
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
, we see that under the above isomorphism
\partial_{f_{{}_{*}}\mathcal{E}}
could be identified with
\tilde{\partial}^{[\bullet]}_{\mathcal{E}}
on
\mathrm{DR}(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})
. Combining with
Eq.

5.4
, we conclude that

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(X^{\operatorname{HT}},\mathcal{E})\xrightarrow{\simeq}\operatorname{fib}(\mathrm{DR}(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})\xrightarrow{\tilde{\partial}_{\mathcal{E}}^{[\bullet]}}\mathrm{DR}(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})),

hence finish the proof by
Corollary

5.1.8
.
∎

Next we proceed to show that
\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by the
\mathcal{I}^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, generalizing
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.5.16]
. A key ingredient is the geometry of
X^{\operatorname{HT}}
studied in
[
BL22b
]
and
[
AHLB23
]
, which is summarized as
[
AHLB23
, Corollary 3.11]
in our setting.

Lemma 5.1.14

(
[
AHLB23
, Corollary 3.11]
)
.

Let
G_{X}
be the group sheaf of automorphisms
\mathrm{Aut}(\rho_{X})
of
\rho_{X}:X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)\to X^{\operatorname{HT}}
, the restriction of
\rho_{X}
to the Hodge-Tate locus. Then

G_{X}\cong(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m+1}=\prod_{i=0}^{m}\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}.

Moreover, the group structure on
G_{X}
transfers through this to the map

\prod_{i=0}^{m}\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}\times\prod_{i=0}^{m}\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp},\quad((c_{i})_{i=0,\cdots,m},(a_{i})_{i=0,\cdots,m})\mapsto(a_{k}+c_{k}(1+ea_{0}))_{k=0,\cdots,m}

with
e
defined to be the image of
d^{\prime}(q)
in
\mathcal{O}_{K}
as usual.

Proof.

This is
[
AHLB23
, Corollary 3.11]
. Note that by unwinding all of the constructions in
loc.cit.
,
E^{\prime}(\pi)
there can be replaced with
d^{\prime}(q)
if one works with the
q
-prism instead of the Breuil-Kisin prism.
∎

As
\psi:\tilde{R}\to S
constructed in
Lemma

5.1.3
reduces to the natural embedding after modulo
d
, we see
\gamma_{b,c_{\psi}}
constructed in
Proposition

5.1.4
descends to an automorphism of

X^{\operatorname{HT}}\times_{\mathrm{Spf}(R)}\mathrm{Spf}(R\oplus_{i=0}^{m}R\epsilon_{i}),

which implies that for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
,
\partial_{\mathcal{E}},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}
also descends to a functor from
\mathcal{E}
to itself.

Under such an identification,
\gamma_{b,c_{\psi}}
corresponds to an element in
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}(S/d)
, which is precisely
(\epsilon,\epsilon,\cdots,\epsilon)_{0\leq i\leq m}\in(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m+1}
after identifying the latter with
G_{X}
via
Lemma

5.1.14
. Indeed, this follows from the discussion above
Lemma

3.2.1
and
Lemma

4.1.15
. Consequently, we can describe
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}
for
\mathcal{E}=\rho_{X,*}(\mathcal{O}_{X})
very explicitly, which could be viewed as a combination of
Lemma

3.2.2
and
Lemma

4.1.15
.

Lemma 5.1.15
.

Let
\mathcal{E}=\rho_{X,*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
, then

•

\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong R\{a_{0},a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\}^{\wedge}_{p}
.

•

Suppose that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
,then the sequence
0\to(R\{a_{j}\}_{j\neq i})^{\wedge}_{p}\to\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}\xrightarrow{\nabla_{i}}\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}\to 0
is exact for all
0\leq i\leq m
, here
\nabla_{0}=\partial
by abuse of notation.

Proof.

Lemma

5.1.14
and the projection formula tells us that
\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong R\{a_{0},a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\}^{\wedge}_{p}
. For the second statement, the case that
i=0
was already treated in
Lemma

3.2.2
(note that the group formula used there are the same as that stated in
Lemma

5.1.14
). For any
i\geq 1
,
we write
B
for
(R\{a_{j}\}_{j\neq i})^{\wedge}_{p}
and omit
i
from the subscript of
a_{i}
and
T_{i}
for the ease of notation. By
Lemma

5.1.14
, the formal group law on the
i
-th component of
(\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp})^{m}
is given by

\Delta:\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}\hat{\otimes}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}},~~~~a\mapsto a+b(1+ea_{0})

and the isomorphism
\gamma_{b,c_{\psi_{i}}}
(hence also the
q
-derivation
\nabla_{i}
) is constructed via
\epsilon\in\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}(S/d)
, hence for
f(a)=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}c_{i}\frac{a^{n}}{n!}\in\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}=B\{a\}_{p}^{\wedge}
with
c_{i}\in B
,
\gamma_{b,c_{\psi_{i}}}(f)=f(a+\epsilon(1+ea_{0}))=f+\epsilon\nabla_{i}(f)
. If we denote
1+ea_{0}\in B
as
u
for simplicity, then it follows that

\begin{split}\epsilon\nabla_{i}(f)&=f(a+\epsilon u)-f(a)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\frac{(a+\epsilon u)^{n}-a^{n}}{n!}\\
&=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{i}a^{i}\epsilon^{n-i}u^{n-i})=\epsilon\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}(\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\binom{n}{i}a^{i}u^{n-i}(\beta T)^{n-i-1}),\end{split}

where the last equality follows from
\epsilon^{j}=(\beta T)^{j-1}\epsilon
. Consequently

\begin{split}\nabla_{i}(f)&=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}a^{i}(\sum_{n=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{n!}\binom{n}{i}u^{n-i}(\beta T)^{n-1-i})\\
&=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{a^{i}}{i!}(\sum_{n=i+1}^{\infty}\frac{c_{n}}{(n-i)!}u^{n-i}(\beta T)^{n-1-i}).\end{split}

We then consider the infinite dimensional matrix
M
with
M_{i,j}=\frac{u^{j+1-i}(\beta T)^{j-i}}{(j+1-i)!}
for
i\leq j
and
0
otherwise, then
M
is an upper triangular matrix with all the diagonal elements being
u\in B^{\times}
, hence
M
is an invertible matrix. Let
g=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}\frac{a^{n}}{n!}
, then the previous calculation tells us that
\nabla_{i}(f)=g
if and only if

(5.5)

M\vec{c}=\vec{b}.

for
\vec{b}=(b_{0},b_{1},\cdots)^{\mathrm{T}}
and
\vec{c}=(c_{1},c_{2},\cdots)^{\mathrm{T}}
.
As
M
is invertible, given any
\vec{b}
satisfies that
b_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
, there exists a unique
\vec{c}
solving
Eq.

5.5
. Moreover,
c_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
. This finishes the proof of the second result stated in the proposition.
∎

Thanks to
Lemma

5.1.15
, we get the following Poincare lemma for
\mathcal{O}_{X}
.

Corollary 5.1.16
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\rho_{X,*}\mathcal{O}_{X}\in\mathcal{D}(x^{\operatorname{HT}})
. Suppose that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. Then there is a canonical resolution from
R
to the total complex of

\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega^{1}_{R/\bar{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\partial}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega^{m}_{R/\bar{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\partial}^{[m]}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega^{1}_{R/\bar{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\cdots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\otimes_{R}\Omega^{m}_{R/\bar{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0.}

Proof.

Given
Lemma

5.1.15
, the proof is similar to that for
Corollary

4.1.16
. For simplicity, we treat the case that
m=1
, which amounts to showing that the total complex of

\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\partial}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\nabla_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}}
\textstyle{\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}}

is concentrated on degree
0
and is given by
R
. The only nontrivial part is to check that given a pair
(x,y)\in\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}\oplus\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}
such that
\tilde{\partial}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}(x)-\nabla(y)=0
, then there exists
t\in\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}
satisfying that
\nabla(t)=x
and that
\partial(t)=y
. For this purpose, first notice that
\nabla
is surjective by
Lemma

5.1.15
, hence we could find
t_{1}\in\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}_{0}
such that
\nabla(t_{1})=x
. Then
\nabla(y-\partial(t_{1}))=\nabla(y)-\tilde{\partial}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{E}_{0}}\circ\nabla(t_{1})=0
, hence
y-\partial(t_{1})\in\ker(\nabla)
. By the proof of
Lemma

5.1.15
,
\partial:\ker(\nabla)\to\ker(\nabla)
is also surjective, hence there esists
f\in\ker(\nabla)
such that
\partial(f)=y-\partial(t_{1})
, then
t=t_{1}+f
satisfies that
\nabla(t_{1})=x
and that
\partial(t)=y
, we are done.
∎

As a byproduct, we get the following refinement of
Proposition

5.1.13
when
n=1
, which is also an analog of
Proposition

4.1.17
. Moreover,
Proposition

5.1.17
could lead to a direct proof of
Proposition

5.1.13
by standard dévissage.

.

.. For any

Moreover, taking cohomology induces a canonical quasi-isomorphism from

Example 5.1.18

(
q
-Higgs connections and derivations on the Breuil-Kisin twists when restricted to the Hodge-Tate locus)
.

For
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{X^{\operatorname{HT}}}\{k\}
, combining
Example

3.2.4
with
Example

4.1.19
, we see that
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}=0
and
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
is given by multiplication by
e\frac{(1+p^{\alpha+1})^{k}-1}{p^{\alpha+1}}
. Moreover, as in
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.5.14]
,
Proposition

5.1.17
implies that
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\operatorname{HT}}}\{k\}
if and only if
\rho^{*}\mathcal{E}\cong\mathcal{O}_{X}
,
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}=0
and
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
is given by multiplication by
e\frac{(1+p^{\alpha+1})^{k}-1}{p^{\alpha+1}}
.

The resolution above essentially leads to the following desired result.

Proposition 5.1.19
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}(X^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by the
\mathcal{I}^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Arguing as
[
BL22a
, Corollary 3.5.16]
, we are reduced to proving that
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by the
\mathcal{I}^{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. We follow the proof of
[
BL22a
, Proposition 3.5.15]
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
m=1
, which already reflects the non-commutativity of
G_{X}
for which
X^{\operatorname{HT}}=BG_{X}
. For general
m
, a similar argument works. By replacing
\mathcal{E}
with
\rho_{X,*}\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E}
thanks to
Proposition

5.1.17
, it suffices to show that
\rho_{X,*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
lies in the full subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
defined to be the full subcategory generated under shifts and colimits by the
\mathcal{I}^{k}
. By
Lemma

5.1.14
, we can identify
\mathcal{D}(X^{\operatorname{HT}})
with the
\infty
-category of
\mathcal{O}_{G_{X}}
-comodule objects in
\widehat{\mathcal{D}}(R)
. Under this identification,
\rho_{X,*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
corresponds to the
p
-complete regular representation of
\widehat{\mathcal{O}}_{\prod_{i=0}^{1}\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\sharp}}=R\{a_{0},a_{1}\}^{\wedge}_{p}
thanks to
Lemma

5.1.15
. For each
n\geq 0,0\leq k\leq n
, Let
V_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}+k}
denote the
R
-submodule of
R\{a_{0},a_{1}\}^{\wedge}_{p}
generated by
\frac{a_{0}^{i}}{i!}\cdot\frac{a_{1}^{j}}{j!}
for
i+j<n
or
i+j=n,j\leq k
. Then the calculation in the proof of
Lemma

3.2.2
and
Lemma

5.1.15
implies that

\begin{split}\partial(\frac{a_{0}^{n-k}}{(n-k)!}\frac{a_{1}^{k}}{k!})=\frac{a_{0}^{n-k}}{(n-k)!}\frac{a_{1}^{k}}{k!}\cdot(e\frac{(1+p^{\alpha+1})^{n-k}-1}{p^{\alpha+1}})\quad&\text{mod}\quad V_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}+k-1},\\
\nabla(\frac{a_{0}^{n-k}}{(n-k)!}\frac{a_{1}^{k}}{k!})=0\quad&\text{mod}\quad V_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}+k-1}.\end{split}

Invoking
Example

5.1.18
, we obtain fiber sequences

V_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}+k-1}\to V_{\frac{n(n+1)}{2}+k}\to\mathcal{O}_{X^{\operatorname{HT}}}\{n-k\}

It follows by induction on
j
that each
V_{\leq j}
belongs to the category
\mathcal{C}
. Taking colimit on
j
implies that
\rho_{X,*}\mathcal{O}_{X}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}
.
∎

Remark 5.1.20
.

Although we assume that
X
is small affine over
\mathcal{O}_{K}=W(k)[\zeta_{p^{\alpha+1}}]
in this section,
Proposition

5.1.19
holds for such an
X
over a general
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. This follows as
Lemma

5.1.15
holds in this general setting by replacing
\nabla_{i}
with the Higgs field
\theta_{i}
studied in
[
AHLB23
]
(the proof of
Lemma

5.1.15
works verbatimly by replacing
e=d^{\prime}(q)
with
E^{\prime}(\pi)
).

Finally we can state and prove the main results in this section.

Theorem 5.1.21
.

Assume that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
. The functor

\displaystyle\beta_{n}^{+}:\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]),\qquad\mathcal{E}\mapsto(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\partial_{\mathcal{E}},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})

constructed in
Proposition

5.1.12
is fully faithful. Moreover, its essential image consists of those objects
M\in\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])
satisfying the following pair of conditions:

•

M
is
(p,d)
-adically complete.

•

The action of
\partial
and
\nabla_{i}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes_{\tilde{R}/d^{n}}^{\mathbb{L}}\tilde{R}/(d,q-1))
is locally nilpotent for all
i
.

Proof.

The functor is well-defined thanks to
Proposition

5.1.12
and the full faithfulness follows from
Proposition

5.1.13
directly.

To see that the image of
\beta_{n}^{+}
satisfies the stated conditions, we just notice that by
Proposition

5.1.19
, the essential image of
\beta_{n}^{+}
is generated by
\beta_{n}^{+}(\mathcal{I}^{k})
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}
) under shifts and colimits, and the action of
\partial
and
\nabla_{i}
on the underlying module
\rho_{X}^{*}
satisfies the nilpotence condition due to
Example

5.1.18
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{n}\subseteq\mathcal{D}(\tilde{R}/d^{n}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])
be the full subcategory spanned by objects satisfying two conditions listed in
Theorem

4.1.23
. As the source
\mathcal{D}(X_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
is generated under shifts and colimits by the structure sheaf thanks to
Proposition

4.1.22
, to complete the proof it suffices to show that
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is also generated under shifts and colimits by
\beta^{+}_{n}(\mathcal{I}^{k})
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. In other words, we need to show that for every nonzero object
M\in\mathcal{C}_{n}
,
M
admits a nonzero morphism from
\beta^{+}_{n}(\mathcal{I}^{k})[m]
for some
k,m\in\mathbb{Z}
. For this purpose, one can reduce to the Hodge-Tate case first and then copy the proof of
Theorem

4.1.23
by replacing
Remark

4.1.12
with
Corollary

5.1.8
.
∎

5.2.
Locally complete intersection case

In this subsection we would like to classify quasi-coherent complexes on the prismatization of
Y
, for
Y
a locally complete intersection over
\mathcal{O}_{K}
. We still work with
\mathcal{O}_{K}=W(k)[\zeta_{p^{\alpha+1}}]
(
\alpha\geq 0
) and the
q
-prism
(A,I)=(W(k)[[q-1]],[p]_{q^{p^{\alpha}}})
.

As in the last subsection,
X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)
is a small affine over
\mathcal{O}_{K}=W(k)[\zeta_{p^{\alpha+1}}]
(
\alpha\geq 0
) and we fix the following diagram

\textstyle{A\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{\tilde{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bar{A}\left\langle\underline{T}\right\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{R,}

where the horizontal maps are étale chart maps.

As usual, we assume that

(

and

is its image in

) is a closed embedding such that the prismatic envelope with respect to the morphism of

. Main examples for such

are given in

.

To study

\textstyle{Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

.

By

4.2.4
,
Y_{/\tilde{R}}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}

. We then proceed by extending

to

Lemma 5.2.1
.

Let

which will still be denoted as

for any

The proof is the same as that for

4.2.5
.
∎

Remark 5.2.2
.

As the automorphisms
\gamma_{i}
(
0\leq i\leq m
) on

is

belongs to the ideal generated by

in

, running a similar argument as that for

, we see that all of these

’s extend uniquely to automorphisms of

by the universal property of the latter. Moreover, the relations given in Equation (

) and

still holds on

. Then similarly as

5.1.6
, we can define the

{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)}]
.

Given

5.2.1
, we then restrict

5.1.4
to

as follows.

Proposition 5.2.3
.

\gamma_{b,c_{\psi}}
constructed in
Proposition

5.1.4
restricts to an isomorphism
\gamma_{b,c_{\psi}}
between functors
\rho:\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)\xrightarrow{\iota}\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))\xrightarrow{\rho_{Y}}Y^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}

and
\rho\circ\psi:\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))\xrightarrow{\rho_{Y}}Y^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
, i.e. we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)\otimes_{\tilde{R}}S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{c_{\psi}}}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{Y}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{Y}}
\textstyle{Y^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}}

Proof.

By restricting

used in the proof of

5.1.4
to

, we get the desired isomorphism of Cartier-Witt divisors obtained via

separately. The rest follows from the proof of

4.2.8
.
∎

Similarly as the discussion in the previous sections,
Proposition

5.2.3
and
Remark

5.2.2
imply that for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(Y^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
,
\rho_{Y}^{*}\mathcal{E}
is equipped with
\nabla_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\partial_{\mathcal{E}}
satisfying a sequence of relations.
With these preliminaries in hand, we extend
Theorem

5.1.21
to the locally complete intersection case.

Theorem 5.2.4
.

Assume that
p>2
or
\alpha>0
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
, the covering map
\rho_{Y}:\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n})\to Y_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}}
induces a functor

\displaystyle\beta_{n}^{+}:\mathcal{D}(Y_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})\rightarrow\mathcal{D}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}]),\qquad\mathcal{E}\mapsto(\rho_{X}^{*}\mathcal{E},\partial_{\mathcal{E}},\nabla_{\mathcal{E}})

which is fully faithful. Moreover, its essential image consists of those objects
M\in\mathcal{D}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])
satisfying the following pair of conditions:

•

M
is
(p,d)
-adically complete.

•

The action of
\partial
and
\nabla_{i}
on the cohomology
\mathrm{H}^{*}(M\otimes_{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d^{n}}^{\mathbb{L}}{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/(d,q-1))

is locally nilpotent for all
i
.

Proof.

Given
Proposition

5.2.3
and
Remark

5.2.2
, an analogous result of
Proposition

5.1.5
for
\mathcal{E}\in\mathcal{D}(Y_{n}^{{{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}})
still holds, hence
\beta_{n}^{+}
is well-produced. Moreover, the inductive process for proving
Theorem

5.1.21
still works once we show that the theorem holds for
n=1
, i.e. the Hodge-Tate case.

For this purpose, first we notice that
\rho_{X}:X=\mathrm{Spf}(R)\to X^{\operatorname{HT}}
is a cover with automorphism group
G_{X}
by
Lemma

5.1.14
(here
G_{X}
is defined over
X
). Then by restricting the pullback square above
Lemma

5.2.1
to the Hodge-Tate locus, we obtain the following pullback diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{Spf}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{Y}^{\prime}}

\scriptstyle{\rho_{Y}}
\textstyle{X^{\operatorname{HT}}=X/G_{X}=\mathrm{Spf}(R)/G_{X}.}

Here
(G_{X})_{Y}
is the base change of
G_{X}
along the closed embedding

.

Consequently, we have that

(5.6)

\mathcal{D}(Y^{\operatorname{HT}})=\mathrm{Mod}_{\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{D}(X/G_{X})=\mathrm{Mod}_{\beta_{1}^{+}(\pi_{*}\mathcal{O})}(\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{Nil}}({{\mathbbl{\Delta}}}_{X}(Y)/d[\partial,\underline{\nabla};\gamma_{\tilde{R}}])).

Here we use

to denote the essential image of
\beta_{1}^{+}
stated in

5.1.21
for simplicity.

But arguing as that in the proof of

4.2.11
, we conclude that the right-hand side of
Eq.

5.6
is exactly the wanted category in
